#243. How video game remakes tap the power of nostalgia

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is often rated as one of the greatest video games

The moment she saw footage from the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, Kacey Doran picked up her phone. She had to tell her sister.

"Have you seen this?" messaged Doran, excitedly.

Nearly 30 years ago, a 10-year-old Doran had sat in front of her family's TV, reading out the dialogue on screen to her sister, a year younger, as they inched their way through the original Nintendo 64 game's sprawling, puzzle-filled dungeons. Ocarina's fantasy-like story, and beautiful music, enthralled them both.

It was also the first game Doran ever completed. Now, countless memories rushed back to her as she devoured clips of the fully redesigned version, due to arrive on the Switch 2 later this year.

Doran's sister messaged back: "I'm not tearing upâ¦ YOU'RE tearing up."

News of a video game remake bought back happy memories for Kacey Doran

This is the power of nostalgia â and a perfect example of how video games can trigger it. Doran now teaches an esports course at Rowan University in New Jersey. She says the original Ocarina of Time, released in 1998, had a "magical" quality that she hopes to rediscover, and also share with her sister's children.

The video games industry has long leaned on remakes. From Mario Kart to the Final Fantasy series, or even Pac-Man, some games just keep coming back in updated forms, as new generations of gamers emerge and as computer hardware improves, enabling more detailed graphics and increasingly expansive game worlds.

While the emotional hook may be the most significant draw, putting out a remake can be a double-edged sword. Some gamers are very protective of their favourite titles and new versions don't always meet certain fans' astronomically high expectations.

Nostalgia is an important but mixed emotion, says Tim Wulf, at the Centre for Advanced Internet Studies in Bochum, Germany. It has positive aspects but also a kind of sadness, too. Happiness in remembering things that were important to us, and also an awareness that that time is now gone.

"It's a social emotion because memories that induce nostalgia for us are connected to other people," he explains, adding that nostalgia can boost our self-esteem while highlighting key social bonds.

Video games, says Wulf, are notoriously good at triggering nostalgia because gamers can "jump into the same environment". In other words, games are like time machines for our emotions, and returning to fondly remembered places can feel stabilising or reassuring in an ever-changing world.

Doran, for one, notes that when you return to a favourite old game, there's something satisfying about deploying previously learned skills. You know what to do here, you're winning again. Just like the old days.

Video games are good at triggering nostalgic feelings says Tim Wulf

Putting out remasters and remakes has been a "really successful" strategy for the games industry, says Piers Harding-Rolls, head of games research at Ampere Analysis.

"Tying in with established audiences and building on existing content is quite a safe route for a lot of companies," he adds. In many cases, it is cheaper to update an existing game rather than create a new title from scratch.

Among other remakes that have found big audiences recently are those of Resident Evil 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag.

But Ocarina of Time perhaps stands out because, ever since its original release, it has been considered by many as one of the greatest games ever made. "The interest from all the Gen Zs that I work with is really high," says Harding-Rolls. The legend of The Legend of Zelda, as it were.

Since 2007 there have been 14 main releases of Assassin's Creed

Clearly, nostalgia is not the only driver of interest in the remake, and today's audiences will expect a game that plays to today's standards.

Games companies must keep innovating when linking contemporary audiences to the past, says Wulf, in order satisfy their desire for fresh experiences. And although Wulf himself was a big fan of the original, he has no personal interest in the Ocarina of Time remake. "I want a new game â I don't want to play an old game," he says. Nostalgia isn't always enough for some.

Plus, critics often highlight a tension between originals and remakes. Which is the definitive version? In 2021, Square Enix released the bizarrely titled Nier Replicant ver.1.22474487139... â a remaster of a game first published in 2010. One reviewer wrote, external, "What we're left with is two nearly identical Niers each vying to be the true Nier."

Fans may react badly should a remaster or remake fail to do justice to the source material. And, in the gaming world, angry fan reactions can be serious.

"There's always going to be that fan base or group of people that are going to nit-pick," says Stephanie Ijoma, chief executive and founder of NNESAGA, a consultancy and creative agency.

Ultimately, games developers working on remakes have to "find the right balance" between appealing to fans and allowing themselves to be creative with the material, she adds. "You can't make art in fear, you can't create in fear."

Ijoma gives the example of Final Fantasy VII. First released in 1997, it got a highly anticipated remake in 2020.

This was a game Ijoma played back in the late 1990s and she was thrilled to be able to share it with younger players today.

First released in 1997, Final Fantasy VII got a remake in 2020

Remakes enable this sharing of experiences because getting old computer hardware or video games consoles to work, while a pastime loved by some, is not possible for everyone.

"I've tried multiple times to play Silent Hill 2 because I'm interested in older games but it's such a difficult game to come into nowadays," says Niklas Hallin, a solo indie game developer who runs the studio Neckbolt. "They made a remake and it's so smooth, it just works. It works on your modern hardware."

Hallin did not play many games as a child so he says nostalgia isn't much of a motivator for him. However, in his own games, he likes to include subtle stylistic references to classic games because he knows that this appeals to gamers.

Indie developers often lean on retro games aesthetics not just because people respond well to them â but also because they are easier to achieve. "You can't compete with billion dollar budgets, super high-fidelity, Triple A graphics," he says.

One question remains. The visual difference, today, between games released years apart is, arguably, negligible. So will there be any point in remaking a game from 2026 in, say, 2038? Will technology have moved on so much by then that it's actually worth it?

Harding-Rolls predicts that games companies will still seek to connect new audiences with classic titles by offering updates.

Remakes might become less about graphical improvements and more about editorial adjustments. "Reinterpretations," he says. "Or versions with additional content."

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#242. Fed's John Williams indicates higher interest rates likely 'over time' to address inflation

New York Federal Reserve President John Williams said Monday that policymakers have more work to do to bring inflation under control, though he indicated that further action can unfold over time as the economy continues to grow.

In remarks consistent with previous statements, the influential central banker expressed confidence that inflation will get closer to the Fed's 2% target in 2027 before hitting the goal the following year. He also said that while artificial intelligence is pressuring prices, there's "little evidence" it will be the source of more persistent inflation.

"But getting inflation back to 2% is 100% essential," he said in remarks at the University of Mount Saint Vincent in New York. "We must take actions over time that will ensure that we get inflation back to our 2% goal in a timely manner and on a sustained basis. There is still work to do to get that done."

The comments come at a delicate moment for monetary policy, as the economy continues to grow and inflation stays well above the Fed's goal.

Fed officials have differed over how quickly to raise rates, with some advocating more aggressive action while others, including Williams, have emphasized a measured approach.

Williams ascribed the current inflation pressures to the conflict in Iran, demand for goods associated with the AI build-out and tariffs, the effects of which he said "have largely abated" as a source of pressure. Nevertheless, he expressed confidence that inflation will subside substantially in 2027 even as economic growth remains robust and unemployment edges lower.

The Federal Open Market Committee next meets on Dec. 8-9, with markets fully pricing in a quarter percentage point interest rate increase from the current target range of 3.75%-4%. The remarks from Williams are expected to be the last major policy statement from any Fed official before the December decision.

"Monetary policy cannot move ships or reopen pipelines and refineries, but it can — and must — ensure that these shocks do not lead to broader or persistent inflationary dynamics or erode well-anchored inflation expectations," Williams said, according to his prepared remarks.

Williams did not explicitly address the December meeting expectations, instead pointing to the statement released after the prior FOMC meeting in late October that said the "accumulation of more information" will help decide the future policy path.

"The data that we have received since then show that the economy has remained strong and that inflation has continued to run too high," he said.

#238. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

#237. Target shares rise 7% as retailer posts best sales growth in five years, hikes guidance

Target on Wednesday reported its best sales growth in more than five years and beat Wall Street's revenue expectations, the latest signs of its turnaround strategy taking hold.

The retailer said its net sales grew 7.1% during the fiscal third quarter. Comparable sales rose 5.6%, compared with StreetAccount estimates of up 3.8%. That growth was boosted by a 5% increase in comparable traffic, Target added.

"[It's] a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often," CEO Michael Fiddelke told reporters.

Digital comparable sales jumped nearly 10% during the quarter.

Encouraged by the growth, Target raised its full-year guidance, upping its net sales growth guidance by 1 percentage point to around 6%. It's also expecting full-year operating income margin rate to be around 6.2%, which includes roughly 1.1 percentage points of benefit from tariff refunds.

The company also said it's now projecting full-year earnings per share to be in a range of $10.65 to $11.40, compared with prior guidance of between $9.90 and $10.90.

"The changes we've made across our assortment and guest experience are adding up," Fiddelke told reporters. "While we're encouraged by the progress, we know there's more work ahead to sustain this momentum and deliver consistent growth over time."

Target shares climbed about 7% in afternoon trading Wednesday.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 31, Target reported net income of $1.21 billion, or $2.65 per share, up from $689 million, or $1.51 per share, a year earlier.

This year, the company said it's invested approximately $3.8 billion in capital expenditures, up nearly $1 billion from last year. Because of those returns, Fiddelke said Target is now expecting to invest between $5 billion and $6 billion in capex next year.

On a call with analysts, he added that those investments will go toward more store remodels, technology, supply chain improvements, among other initiatives.

The company said it saw particular strength in beauty, which saw 9% growth, and food and beverage, where sales increased nearly 7%. The retailer added that net sales rose in all three months of the third quarter, with the strongest performance during the back-to-school season.

Fiddelke told reporters the quarter's strength showed "continued progress" toward Target's priorities that it outlined at the beginning of its turnaround.

As it looks to the holiday season, Target said it's marketing thousands of products priced at $5 and under, along with new artificial intelligence-powered shopping experiences to drive engagement. Fiddelke said Target is "encouraged" by shopping behaviors it's seen so far in the current quarter.

The big-box retailer has been showing signs of returning to growth, as it sees stronger sales trends after years of flat revenue. The results come after Target last quarter hiked its full fiscal-year outlook and said it saw "broad-based" strength across its various categories.

It's also lowered prices on more than 10,000 items in an effort to get customers back into its stores, especially as consumer sentiment lags and inflation remains high. The retailer said on Wednesday that it cut prices on an additional roughly 2,000 items during the third quarter.

Target's stock is up more than 70% this year.

#235. Russia accuses NATO member Estonia of hostile actions ‘on the verge of unleashing open war’

Russia's Foreign Ministry has lashed out at Estonia's government, accusing the NATO member of carrying out a range of "hostile actions" and putting the Baltic state "on the verge of unleashing open war" with Moscow.

The outburst underscores rising tensions between Russia and countries on NATO's eastern flank and comes after Estonia announced it will close its road border crossings with Russia from Dec. 1.

Russia's ministry said Monday it had lodged a formal protest to Estonia's chargé d'affaires, Marek Uhtegi, over Tallinn's decision to close its border crossings and accused the European Union member of imposing a naval blockade against Moscow.

"The Estonian diplomat was again informed that such provocative and extremely dangerous actions are on the verge of unleashing open war with Russia," the ministry said in a statement, describing measures taken by Estonian lawmakers as "Russophobic."

The ministry also accused Estonia of "tacitly" providing its airspace for Ukrainian drone flights and sharply criticized Tallinn's ban on the transit of Russian and Belarusian grain through Estonian territory, referring to a policy that was introduced at the start of October.

Estonia, which shares a 294-kilometer (183-mile) border with Russia, swiftly denied ever allowing its territory to be used for military operations by another country.

It also responded to Russia's allegations by saying the Kremlin appears to be seeking to divert attention from its own efforts to undermine European security.

"Russian missiles have reached Polish territory, Danish security services have identified an assassination plot in Denmark orchestrated by Russian intelligence services, merchant vessels in the Black Sea are under constant threat from Russian attacks, and in Estonia, an arson attack targeting a defence industry company, planned on the orders of Russian military intelligence, was foiled," Estonian Foreign Minister Margus Tsahkna said Monday.

"All this is happening while Russia continues its war of aggression against Ukraine," he added.

The rhetoric marks a significant escalation in tensions between Estonia and Russia. Tallinn, a staunch Ukraine ally, has frequently raised the alarm over sabotage attacks against NATO countries that have been supporting Kyiv since Russia launched the full-scale invasion of its neighbor in February 2022.

Earlier this month, Estonia said it would close road border crossings with Russia from Dec. 1 as it blames Moscow for an attempted "sabotage" of one of its defense companies, an allegation the Kremlin denies.

Tallinn has said the border closure will help to reduce Moscow's ability to conduct hostile activities against it and mitigate risks related to sanctions circumvention.

Ukraine has stepped up its attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Russian President Vladimir Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

The suspected Ukrainian attack on a Russian military-linked cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland on Saturday prompted Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo to warn that the Ukraine war is "spilling over" into its immediate vicinity.

Finland, one of NATO's newest members, has also closed its land border crossings with Russia.

Ukraine's latest attacks on some of Russia's most protected targets have demonstrated Kyiv's capabilities to hurt Moscow even without ample U.S. military support, according to Mario Bikarski, senior Europe and Central Asia analyst for Verisk Maplecroft, a political risk consultancy.

"Ukraine will continue to look for ways to degrade not only Russia's economy, but also its geostrategic interests in the wider region, including the Mediterranean and the Middle East," Bikarski said.

The prospect of an imminent peace deal between Russia and Ukraine appears to be slim, Bikarski said, although "the latest surge in diplomatic activity and mounting losses for both sides signals an appetite from all sides to reach an agreement."

#233. The engine parts that 'fight the laws of the universe'

A jet engine will have hundreds of turbine blades

Every time you fly, an extraordinary feat of endurance occurs inside the aircraft's engines.

Hidden from view inside the sleek outer nacelle are hundreds of little turbine blades that can shrug off enormous mechanical stresses and temperatures exceeding 1,500C.

It is a true engineering marvel. And these hardy blades are not sculpted or cut from their material â they are grown.

During the 1960s, engineers worked out how to take a molten metal alloy, cast it into the shape of a turbine blade, and then cool it very carefully from one end to the other over a matter of hours. A single crystal or grain of metal alloy forms during this process of gradual, delicate solidification.

Like turning a cup of water into one big snowflake, of your own design, rather than a mini blizzard of many smaller snowflakes.

"It's about as complicated as manufacturing gets," says Ben Fifoot, manufacturing engineer at Rolls-Royce, which makes turbine blades in the UK. "It's a real motivator to get out of bed in the morning."

Only a handful of companies around the world can produce these single crystal turbine blades, and the process has been perfected end-to-end in just five countries, including the US and UK. Engine-makers continually vie for access to the technology.

Rolls-Royce, GE and France's Safran are among the few firms that have perfected the process.

In September, GE Aerospace announced, external it would acquire its turbine blade supplier for $11.75bn (Â£8.78bn). Neither company responded to BBC News' requests for comment but the move has been described as an effort to ensure supply of these critical components.

Separately, in March, Rolls-Royce said it would invest more than Â£19m in its Rotherham blade casting facility.

Turbine blade specialists continue to work on improving the capabilities of this little-known technology. All the while, China is desperate to prove that its engineers can produce turbine blades of the same quality as those in the West. Who will come out on top?

"Demand for [turbine blades] is huge. So few companies have managed to do it, which does give it this kind of magical aura," says Bill Sweetman, an aviation industry writer and consultant.

He suspects GE Aerospace's recent move is about securing reliability and predictability in the notoriously volatile aircraft manufacturing supply chain.

The casts play a crucial part in the cooling process

But why is the single crystal casting process, which has been around for decades now, still so specialist?

To grow one of these blades, asserts Fifoot, is to "fight the laws of the universe".

This is because each molten blade would naturally cool into a solid object consisting of many small grains of metal alloy, all jumbled together. But you don't want a crumbly sugar cube, you want a perfect diamond.

Getting a single crystal to grow into the shape of the blade instead isn't just a matter of cooling the molten version down slowly, the process also uses a bizarre trick called a pigtail.

The pigtail is a small spiral at one end of the blade. Fifoot says that, at the beginning of the cooling process, this spiral "chokes" the flow of solidifying crystals so that just one takes over. A bit like a hairpin corner in an F1 race right before the finish line â one driver can take the lead here and go on to win.

"Sometimes we need to play around with the number of turns or the angles on [the pigtail] to try and get more favourable grain," adds Fifoot. Once the blade is formed, this crucial but sacrificial spiral is cut off.

France's Safran is among the few companies that can make single crystal blades

Blades made by Rolls-Royce are used in aircraft engines including the Trent XWB-84, which powers the Airbus A350-900, and the Trent 1000 XE, for the Boeing 787.

The single crystal is essential to ensure each blade's performance. Sometimes a blade doesn't cool quite as planned, for example two crystals form within it instead of just one, and the metal alloy must be reprocessed so that the manufacturer may re-use the material.

Special ceramic coatings also protect blades from extreme temperatures inside engines. And another important feature is that blades are cast with tiny channels that allow a flow of cooler air to coat them in a kind of film while the aircraft engine is working.

"The drive at the moment [across the industry] is currently to make those cooling passages really intricate," says Paul Withey, at the University of Birmingham, who formerly worked for Rolls-Royce. The more turbulent those little flows of cool air are, he explains, the more they will knock heat out of the surrounding, much hotter air.

And blade-makers also seek to reduce the mass of these components as much as possible.

"If we can take any weight out of it, you can run that engine more efficiently using less fuel â you can spin it faster and hotter," says Fifoot.

The turbine blades are used in the Trent 1000 engine among others

Meanwhile, China hopes to become only the sixth country to master the whole technology chain for single crystal blade casting.

A report in May, external quoted one engineer at the AECC Beijing Institute of Aeronautical Materials claiming that China now had a single crystal alloy "superior to or equivalent to the second-generation single-crystal superalloys widely used in Europe and the US". It costs less to make, too, apparently.

But the West has a "huge advantage" over China given that the majority of commercial airliner production happens there, says Sweetman. Supply chains and the latest innovations are generally clustered in the West. Plus, Western manufacturers also have lengthy safety records under their belts.

China must compete on practically every aspect of aircraft manufacturing, external â by no means just on turbine blades â in order to make serious in-roads. "You can definitely see that happening but it's a long, uphill struggle," adds Sweetman.

Demand for aircraft is currently high, which is already straining various supply chains in the industry. That demand could grow higher still, suggests Withey.

During the next few years, one of the two main aircraft manufacturers, Airbus and Boeing, could launch a new short-haul plane, external, he says. Then, the other would have to follow suit in order to remain competitive.

"There'll be a bun fight," says Withey, over who gets to supply the engines and, by extension, those all-important single crystal turbine blades.

#231. Nvidia shares fall more than 5% as Huang comeback rally cools

Nvidia shares fell 5.86% on Tuesday, paring a massive Monday rally that was sparked by the return of Jensen Huang as CEO.

Other U.S. semiconductor stocks also traded lower after posting strong increases Monday. Micron declined 10.97%, Marvell Technology lost 9.10%, Arm Holdings dropped 8.55% and AMD shed 8.86%.

Neoclouds gave back some of their Monday gains, with CoreWeave losing 11.84%, Iren declining 11.54% and Nebius shedding 10.74%.

Huang, who resigned as CEO of Nvidia earlier this month after admitting to an extramarital affair, was reinstated on Monday. The stock closed over 30% higher following the news of Huang's return.

The company's board of directors said it unanimously voted to reinstate Huang following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him.

President Donald Trump also claimed he was personally involved in Huang's return. The president told CNBC that he spoke with the chipmaker's board before it reversed course and reinstated its co-founder.

"I made the deal," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers on Monday during an interview on "Squawk on the Street."

Trump said Nvidia's board asked for guarantees that Huang, who resigned less than two weeks ago, was not a threat to national security.

"And we gave them assurances, at least with everything we know," the president said. "We believe he's fine. He's absolutely fine."

Many Nvidia employees welcomed Huang's reinstatement, with multiple floors of employees greeting the CEO with cheers Monday at Nvidia's headquarters. In an employee meeting Tuesday, Huang struck a somber tone and acknowledged the impact of his actions. In the first such meeting since his return, Huang doubled down on his stance regarding succession planning, saying Nvidia's unique business model doesn't require it, according to people inside the room.

After Huang's reinstatement, several Wall Street analysts turned more bullish on the stock and upgraded their ratings on Nvidia.

"We believe reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang RETURNS Investor CONFIDENCE in the AI trade," Mizuho analysts wrote in a note on Monday.

Huang and Nvidia have been central to the AI story, supplying the chips that power AI and financing companies across the ecosystem.

The tech giant remains under scrutiny as questions remain about Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy.

The U.S. is investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed, the company where she worked. Questions also remain regarding whether Nvidia chips are making their way into China despite export controls.

Huang is set to publicly testify in front of the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, who is set to take over the Committee in January, called Huang's reinstatement "reckless."

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Warren said in a statement on Monday.

Huang is also expected to sit for a closed-door interview with the House Foreign Affairs, Intelligence and China Select committees on Dec. 1. The hearing falls on the same day Huang, before his resignation, was scheduled to give the keynote address at Nvidia's annual GTC conference in Washington, D.C.

"Reinstatement should not be confused with the end of scrutiny," Bank of America said in a note on Monday. "We therefore expect elevated attention around governance, China/export controls, customer relationships and compliance to persist even with the CEO question resolved."

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Seema Mody and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#227. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#223. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#222. Does the Nvidia-led rip mark the market bottom? Maybe not

The key accelerant to Monday's ripping rebound was a shocker. But the level that halted the rally was right where you'd expect.

The S&P 500's 4.7% surge on Monday lifted it out of a steep-sided hole following last week's washout selling storm. Its high for the day was just under 7300, a level last seen on Thursday, corresponding to the index's summertime low and representing the floor of the former multi-month trading range. In other words, the pop stopped at the point from which the tape had broken down; the index slipped three-quarters of a percent from there by the close.

More than a third of the S&P's gain was provided by the 31% jump in Nvidia shares alone following Jensen Huang's stunning reinstatement as Nvidia CEO. Here, too, the stock closed right near the Iran-selloff low, which it cracked through some 12 days ago. It remains under all the relevant moving averages and trend lines.

This isn't to minimize or denigrate these moves of rare magnitude, but simply to place them in context. The market was going to attempt a solid bounce no matter the news. S&P 500 futures were indicated higher by more than 2% even before the Huang bombshell struck before Monday's open.

By near-universal agreement, last week's flush had generated the kind of indiscriminate liquidation, oversold conditions and urgent hedging that typically set up at least a snapback rip. Some of these readings were as extreme as near the April 2025 low following the tariff panic, though not all.

The reinstallation of Huang at the apparent behest of the White House triggered an emotional, spring-loaded ramp in the AI trade, a collective panic by the under-invested. When the government jolts the market with a policy shift like this, it not only assuages a prevailing fear in the market, but it grants psychic permission to investors who were frantically selling one day to ravenously buy back the same stocks the next.

It's slightly ironic, given that a common line last week on the recent market setback was that – unlike in the tariff panic or the Iran war corrections – there was no single policy reversal that could short-circuit the selloff and trigger a ferocious recovery.

And maybe there still isn't. Such one-day comebacks from a deeply oversold state are to be respected but not fully trusted. Looking back at prior one-day gains of such amplitude – or prior sequences of a daily drop and gain of more than 5% in the Nasdaq-100 – generate a fair number of episodes inside of nasty bear markets, along with a few consequential market bottoms.

A few further observations and questions at a potentially tenuous tactical moment for the markets:

-The standard playbook is to assume that we've put in "a low, not the final low." Oversold rallies can carry on a while – say, to the 20-day moving average, which for the S&P 500 is up another 3% from here – without getting escape velocity. Graybeards argue for keeping the market on a short leash, expecting there will at least be a retest sequence. Prudent to assume so, though it's inescapable that V-shaped bottoms have become a bit more common in recent years, as recently as at the end of March, on the merest hint that President Trump would consider ceasing Iran hostilities without forcing open the Strait of Hormuz.

-Index corrections of at least 10% that happen quickly have generally been more associated with temporary bull-market dips than the start of nastier downturns. This latest S&P 500 correction happened over 33 trading days, among the dozen quickest. That would be reassuring, except that places it right between the declines of November 2007 and February 2022, which indeed were the start of bear markets.

-Remember that the pain of this setback was inflicted after months in which Wall Street was constantly extolling the virtues of a "broadening" market (equal-weight S&P 500 outperforming) and marveling at the elegant rotation into "quality" and defensive stocks that was supporting the S&P 500. Those mechanics can work fine in phases, but never allow for a proper market-wide reset and, clearly, they don't inoculate the tape from eventual washouts.

-We might have a new binary trading relationship to watch: It's early in the game, but with the Nvidia news jolting most AI plays higher, Anthropic in its third trading session since its IPO fell 1.4%. If Nvidia is an advocate for and backer of global open-weight models, then its revival could dim the view of Anthropic's closed frontier AI platform, while diminishing the advantage provided by Anthropic's $200 billion cash capex war chest.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "The growing evidence of capitulation is encouraging. Bearish sentiment was arguably the market's best attribute coming into last week and still is. Sentiment became even more extreme over the past week, pushing our model to its lowest reading since the 2025 bottom, and reaching levels comparable to what we saw during the 2022 bear market. The six-month outlook has improved, but whether this is a V bottom thanks to the return of Jensen Huang, or the market undergoes basing and a possible retest, remains unclear."

-I'm making my way through "The AGI Chronicles: The Inside Story of the Race to Create an Artificial Superintelligence," the new book by former New York Times technology writer Kevin Roose, a smart, engaging and brisk history of AI development and the personalities driving it. For a shortcut through the material, Roose's discussion on the New Yorker Radio Hour podcast is worth a listen.

-In returning to Nvidia headquarters Monday without his signature leather jacket, Jensen Huang was executing a Reverse Fonzie.

In the first season of "Happy Days," the mega-hit sitcom of the '70s and early '80s, ABC forced the Fonz character to wear a cloth windbreaker, fearing he'd alienate viewers who'd take him for a "hoodlum." Creator Garry Marshall argued to the network that a leather jacket was a safety requirement for motorcycle riders and pushed to let him wear. When the show took off, the leather became standard.

Huang is now perhaps trying to seem a bit less edgy, humble and relatable absent the biker attire.

President Trump's direct intervention to press Nvidia's board to reinstate Huang, juicing the Nasdaq for at least a day, reintroduced investors to the notion that stocks can only fall so far without the administration trying to punish the sellers.

But can Wall Street enjoy both a Trump put and a Fed put? If conviction about the AI trade and its aggressive spending is revived, and the economy runs hotter, and private-sector borrowing rises, will that not invite a more hawkish Federal Reserve – at just the time that many were handicapping a dovish pivot given the unsettled markets of late?

It's a live question. More than a month ago, as a fourth-quarter post-election rally was becoming consensus, I granted the setup but with a key caveat:

"Still, against most probabilities, 2018 happened. By which I mean a 19% S&P 500 peak-to-trough decline in the fourth quarter of a midterm-election year, one that saw total S&P 500 earnings grow by more than 20%. The 2018 tailspin had followed a massive momentum crescendo and reversal several months earlier, which gave way to an erratic tape that couldn't absorb what seemed at the time relatively unthreatening Federal Reserve tightening actions."

Since then, after the market retrenchment, the 2018 analogy has grown so popular that I doubt it will unfold exactly that way. Still, as Renaissance Macro noted after Monday's rally, Nvidia's gain, which helped the S&P 500 recoup half of last week's loss, "may have fixed the S&P 500 loss but not the Fed, the [Treasury] curve or cash credit."

Higher real yields remain a potential constraint on equity valuations. This chart from Wellington Management shows the yield on 30-year Treasury Inflation Protected Securities – the long-term real yield – is now above the S&P 500's free-cash-flow yield for the first time in half a generation.

In a related vein, JPMorgan economist Bruce Kasman in his 2027 outlook describes a certain narrative dissonance: "Financial markets have projected strong earnings expectations, in part, because central banks have preached immaculate disinflation and been supportive of easy access to credit. The 2027 landscape — of above-potential growth, a sixth year of elevated inflation, and a renewed labor market tightening — poses a meaningful challenge to the magical thinking held by markets and central banks."

#220. Europe plans to catch the next computer chip wave

Photonic chips use light instead of electrons to process data

"Photonic chips are on the verge of a breakthrough," says Ton van Mol of the Dutch research institute TNO.

If van Mol is right â and TNO, the European Union and the Dutch government are backing the idea with millions of euros - then the technology could be a gamechanger.

The chips which run today's society, from your phone and TV, to the vast data centres meeting our insatiable desire for AI, are mostly electrical.

They process data by shunting electrons around tiny circuits â a single microchip produced today can contain billions of transistors, and sizes are measured in nanometres (one nanometre is a billionth of a metre).

Because they use light instead of electricity, photonic chips are faster, use less energy and generate less heat than traditional computer chips.

Van Mol hopes for an acceleration in photonics similar to the one seen in electrical chips since the 1970s.

"With optical chips, you are now where electronic chips were maybe 40 [or] 50 years ago."

American, Taiwanese and Chinese firms dominate the computer chip industry, but Europe sees the emergence of photonic chips as a fresh opportunity to lead, on what could be, a key future technology.

Which is why the European Union has committed â¬400m ($450m; Â£330m) to a project called PIX Europe, external, with the stated goal of making the continent "a global leader in emerging technology for Photonic Integrated Chips".

"Europe said 'we need to have our own manufacturing in-house'," says van Mol, who is leading a key part of the programme.

"We cannot depend like in the past that everything is manufactured in the US or China - that's not wise, so the [European] Chips Act was called into life, and one of the parts of the act is to build pilot lines to really bring the technology, which is now in labs, to the next stage - manufacturing."

Electronic and photonic chips are produced on wafers made up of hundreds of individual chips. Factories with machines that can handle bigger wafers can churn out chips more efficiently.

"[Photonic chip] manufacturing worldwide is [currently] done on wafers which are four inches (10cm) in size, and the next step is to scale-up production to a six-inch wafer," says van Mol.

His team are already building a 10,000 sq m factory to produce six-inch indium phosphide wafers on the High Tech Campus in the Dutch city of Eindhoven.

It's due to fully open in 2030 and will be a major step forward, not just for photonics in Europe, but around the world, according to van Mol.

"That's the development TNO is going to do, in a pilot line which is really serious.

"In 2030, we hope that we have a stable, reproducible, high-yield process, and then the intention is that the pilot line [opens] to industrial parties who can make commercial production out of it."

Netherland's based ASML is a crucial supplier of chip-making equipment

Europe does have one crucial player in the current computer chip industry - ASML.

The Netherlands-based tech giant is the only company in the world able to build the machines needed to make the most sophisticated semiconductors.

Which is why ASML products will sit at the heart of the pilot line in Eindhoven - it's no coincidence that van Mol's project is based around the corner from ASML's headquarters.

"The way you produce these [photonic] chips is very similar to the way you produce electrical or electronic chip," says van Mol.

"You use the same type of equipment, you also need a high-end clean room, you also use wafers, so the production method is very similar to the traditional semiconductor technology."

ASML itself is keen to explore the opportunities that the emerging technology of photonics could offer.

"Europe has a strong position in photonics research. The next challenge is translating those innovations into industrial-scale manufacturing," says Stanislas Baron, who is the head of ASML's EU/US Customer Team.

Peter Wennik says TNO's pilot production line is a "launchpad" for European tech

ASML's unique success has helped build an influential and lucrative network of companies and institutions devoted to semiconductor technology in and around Eindhoven, and it's a model for what Europe is trying to achieve with photonic chips.

The Eindhoven project has the backing of former ASML chief executive Peter Wennink, author of an influential report for the Dutch government called The Roadmap to Future Prosperity, external, which noted that the market for photonics was expected to grow 10 to 20 times in the next 10 years.

In a news release backing the new pilot line, Wennink described it as "a launchpad for Europe's future digital economy".

"By investing in scalable production capacity, we are creating an ecosystem that can help shape the global technological direction," he said.

Of course, Europe is not alone in looking to the potential of photonics to earn a global economic advantage.

In June this year, US photonics company Coherent announced what it called "the world's first and largest volume-production six-inch indium phosphide manufacturing platform" at its plant in Sherman, in Texas.

The expansion is backed by chip manufacturing giant Nvidia, external, as well as $50 million public funds under the US Chips Act.

It's an illustration of how quickly the market is moving, and how central photonic chips could be to the continuing race to retain an influential stake in AI and technological development more generally.

Europe's bid to establish itself as a global leader in photonic chips â exemplified by the Eindhoven project â is the kind of strategy that makes sense in a globalised economy, says Joris Teer, policy analyst at the European Institute for Security Studies in Brussels.

If the continent can take a lead in photonic chips, then future developments in AI and data will partly be dependent on Europe.

"Europe knows that its high energy and labour costs, skilled workforce shortages, and lack of private and public capital make an across-the-board reindustrialisation strategy impossible", Teer says.

"To retain influence, ensuring dependence on European parties rather than European autonomy is key."

Once up and running, the Eindhoven project will provide a pilot production line which can be used multiple companies.

It will be open to businesses across Europe and beyond, who can design and build chips without having to develop their own expensive production facilities.

"What we want to establish here is what we call a foundry model; there's no intention to have our own product," Ton van Mol says.

Instead, they will produce to order for outside customers.

It's the same idea which, at huge scale, has brought Taiwanese-based giant TSMC a position of global dominance in the manufacture of microchips.

Photonic chips are already in use, in highly specialised areas in data centres and in Lidar (light detection and ranging) sensor technology, for instance.

For van Mol of TNO, helping the industry to scale up, by easing production challenges and increasing capacity, is the key step to moving them into the tech we use in our daily lives, like phones and laptops, and to drive innovation in areas like medical scanners and 6G communications.

"The demand is there", says van Mol, "and the end goal is to have, in Europe, manufacturing of these chips, which is strategically very important because these chips [could] become the backbone of all data and telecom."

#216. Hims & Hers launches new peptide treatments — while admitting there's more to learn about efficacy

Hims & Hers Health on Wednesday said it will offer five formulations of peptides and longevity treatments compounded in the U.S. as part of the initial rollout of its Peptides+ platform, all of which are not approved by regulators.

Even as it announced the new products, Hims & Hers acknowledged it has more to learn about how best to use the peptides and how effective they are — underscoring the uncertainty surrounding the growing space.

The new offerings come after the federal government eased restrictions on compounding certain unapproved peptides, which are short chains of amino ​acids. Those products have exploded in popularity among fitness and longevity enthusiasts who attest that they help with muscle growth, rapid injury recovery and anti-aging, even though most lack evidence from robust human trials.

Hims in late October launched an early access list for the upcoming peptide offerings, and more than 10,000 people signed up within 15 days, a company spokesperson said in a statement.

None of the company's specific compounded products are approved by the Food and Drug Administration. But Hims said they are authorized by law and formulated by pharmacies "that adhere to rigorous quality standards and are licensed and inspected by state boards of pharmacy."

The products include compounded formulations of sermorelin, a peptide that stimulates growth-hormone release and is marketed for recovery, body composition and anti-aging; glutathione, a peptide promoted ‌for anti-aging and athletic recovery; and nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide, or NAD+, a coenzyme that plays a role in cellular metabolism.

The company will also offer compounded versions of vitamin B-12, a vitamin needed to make red blood cells, build DNA, and maintain healthy nerve function, and L-carnitine, an amino acid derivative that helps the body turn fat into energy. The FDA has approved certain prescription forms of both peptides for established medical uses, primarily for diagnosed deficiencies in those compounds.

Hims said it plans to add more peptides and therapies made in the U.S. over time. That includes BPC-157, an unapproved peptide derived from a protein found in human gastric juice that is marketed for healing processes. The company also aims to introduce another unapproved peptide called TB-500, which is derived from a naturally occurring protein in the body and is marketed for broader, systemic soft-tissue recovery and flexibility. Most of the research on both has been conducted in animals, not humans.

To receive the products, members on the Hims platform will start with an assessment of health history and goals with a licensed provider trained in peptide therapies, who can tailor a personalized plan to their needs, if appropriate.

The company said it intends to use its communication platform to check in with patients and ensure they are seeing desired results.

"If they're not, we need to do something about it," Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, Hims chief medical officer, told CNBC. "We need to, you know, adjust the dose when that's appropriate, or in some cases consider other compounds that's maybe a better fit. And of course, there will also be cases when we need to refer people to other care options."

However, Vinjamoori said there's still uncertainty over how to best use the peptides.

"We don't have all of this data that we're used to having with compounds like this," he said. "We have the opportunity to get it right, and I think we see that as our responsibility with a company of our size, our skill, and resources to really lead."

Vinjamoori added that people who have serious illnesses, such as cardiovascular disease, liver disease and kidney disease, will not be candidates for the compounds for now.

Meanwhile, Andrew Huberman, a well known neuroscientist, podcaster and Stanford University School of Medicine professor who recently joined Hims' board of directors, in a statement called compounds "real medical treatments, not just a wellness trend." He said Hims provides safe access through "trusted sourcing and physician oversight."

But health experts raised concerns about the broader mainstream marketing of peptides in the U.S. and accessing them on telehealth platforms.

"With peptides, I think you have to view them the same way you would view any drug that hasn't been rigorously tested," said Joshua Kritzer, a chemistry professor at Tufts University. "Without actual testing, all you have is anecdotal information, and that's just not enough information to put a substance in your body."

Kritzer said the lack of large clinical trials means researchers can't rule out potentially serious, rare complications from peptides — the kinds of risks that have led regulators to reject or pull otherwise effective drugs from the market after they underwent comprehensive testing in humans.

Without that testing, it's also unclear how telehealth firms, physicians and pharmacies can make decisions about what doses patients should take, said Paul Knoepfler, a cell biology and human anatomy professor at UC Davis Health. While some people rely on anecdotal data from real peptide users, "the dosing for these products really has not been established," he added.

Knoepfler said one risk is that injecting too much of a synthetic peptide into patients could trigger "weird immune responses that are really bad for one's health" because the body might recognize it as foreign.

"With the peptides, it's very loosey-goosey," Knoepfler told CNBC. "How does one even know what would be the effective amount for whatever you're hoping it will do, and what amount would have more risks?"

But he said the more contact patients can have with physicians around taking a compounded peptide, the better. Providers, including those tied to telehealth firms, should make sure patients are well-informed about the possible risks and unknowns of a given product and consider their preexisting conditions, cancer history and other medications, Knoepfler said.

That appears to be consistent with the approach Hims & Hers is taking. The company says patients who use the peptide and longevity products can also use its Care+ offering, which pairs treatment with ongoing support. That includes twice-a-year lab testing to track their progress and the ability to message providers trained in peptide therapy around the clock with any questions.

Still, Kritzer argued that having a doctor who only knows a patient from online, essentially mail-order platforms or websites "is not enough supervision to put you on a prescription medication," especially an unapproved one.

Hims' ability to legally sell some of these peptides stems from a major shift in federal oversight by the Health and Human Services Department. It issued a policy in October that says the Food and Drug Administration will not take enforcement action against licensed pharmacies, federal facilities or physicians that compound and dispense six unapproved peptides while formal rules are reviewed.

In practice, that means these pharmacies and other firms can now offer them, even though the peptides aren't yet on the FDA's official list of ingredients compounding pharmacies are allowed to make. It does not translate to formal approval of the specific peptides.

The policy followed a 2023 decision by the Biden administration where the FDA restricted compounding pharmacies from producing popular peptide formulations due to safety, impurity and immune-reaction concerns.

HHS Secretary ​Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has supported broader access to peptides, calling himself a "big fan" of the treatments and arguing that restrictions were driving Americans to potentially unsafe products from overseas. Kennedy has previously described using BPC-157 to treat a muscle injury.

The announcement also comes after Hims in February 2025 acquired a California-based peptide manufacturing facility equipped with advanced peptide synthesizers.

The evidence for peptide efficacy varies by compound.

A 2026 systematic review screening 113 studies, including both small trials on humans along with rodent research, found zero eligible clinical outcome trials proving that administering IV or intramuscular NAD+ improves anti-aging, energy, or wellness endpoints in humans.

There is some evidence for specific uses of certain peptides, such as potential weight loss. A review of 37 L-carnitine trials found participants lost about 2.7 pounds on average, though that analysis does not demonstrate that a compounded injection works the same way.

Meanwhile, vitamin B-12 injections are most commonly used to help people with a diagnosed deficiency rather than preventative care. U.S. survey data shows only 3.6% of adults are B-12 deficient.

Glutathione's evidence is thin, too. IV injections of it are widely marketed for skin whitening, yet not FDA-approved for this purpose.

A small study published in the Journal of Pakistan Association of Dermatologists evaluating IV glutathione showed that the treatment is "not very effective" for skin-lightening, and that it loses efficacy with time.

The FDA also said in August that it received reports of at least 30 patients who experienced adverse events after they received compounded IV glutathione alone or in combination with other ingredients from different compounding pharmacies.

All of the glutathione tied to the reports were from the same wholesale distributor and was labeled as dietary supplement grade, which is not an appropriate ingredient to make injectable drugs. Two of the Texas pharmacies conducted recalls related to elevated endotoxin levels in their glutathione injectable drugs.

These studies and reports don't speak to how Hims' exact combination will perform, but they do highlight the current limits on data studying the treatments and the questions surrounding them.
