#215. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

#212. Ford starts U.S. battery cell production using Chinese tech amid geopolitical tensions

MARSHALL, Mich. — Ford Motor has started producing battery cells in the U.S. for its next generation of electric vehicles using technologies licensed by Chinese battery giant CATL.

The automaker said Wednesday that cell production at a $3 billion plant in Marshall, Michigan — roughly 100 miles west of Detroit — started last month and the cells are now shipping out. They'll be used in prototypes for its Fathom small electric pickup truck, which CEO Jim Farley has touted as rivaling Tesla and Chinese competitors.

The production marks a milestone for the Detroit automaker and is the next step toward using lower-cost lithium iron phosphate, or LFP, battery cells in U.S. vehicles. The technology was created in the U.S. but commercialized largely in China.

The automaker's decision to make the batteries at the roughly 2 million-square-foot facility has been criticized by some lawmakers and U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over Ford's ties to CATL.

Alan Clarke, Ford's vice president of advanced development projects, said licensing the technology from CATL helped the automaker bring the low-cost battery chemistry to the U.S. as quickly as possible.

"If we want to get to being able to produce affordable EVs in the United States, we need to also do it the fastest way possible," Clarke said Tuesday during a tour of the facility. "CATL being the biggest in the world means that they're the best supplier to get us that technology and help us learn ourselves how to produce LFP cells."

The U.S. government has increased scrutiny of Chinese ties with U.S. companies even as President Donald Trump is meeting this week with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

In September, Duffy sent a letter to Farley expressing "profound concern" about Ford's ties to Chinese companies that it believes could be detrimental to the Detroit carmaker and the U.S. automotive industry. The White House a day later followed up by commending the automaker for its domestic jobs and manufacturing.

Ford — the largest producer of U.S. vehicles — has routinely touted its American roots, jobs and manufacturing, especially as the Biden and Trump administrations have pushed to onshore and grow U.S. production.

Ford's battery plant in Marshall is expected to be capable of producing 40 battery cells a minute, equating to 20 gigawatt-hours annually for EVs and the company's energy storage business. That's down from initial plans of up to 40 gigawatt-hours annually amid an EV pullback by the automaker and broader industry.

Ford has said the production pace will help reduce battery costs and that the facility has room for additional capacity if needed.

The facility is highly automated, but Ford said it's still in the early stages of certifying its processes, as it plans to ramp up cell production next year before the expected launch of the Fathom pickup during the fourth quarter of 2027.

#211. San Francisco is enjoying an AI boom, but worries about a crash are growing

The city of San Francisco is the heart of the AI sector

Lauren Goldboss is busy. The owner of a small San Francisco dog-walking company, she has been hiring staff and buying vans.

Fuelling the growth is her new office dog-walking service Paw Perk, popular with some local AI start-ups.

Her business is an example of how the AI boom in San Francisco is benefiting other sectors.

The city saw an exodus of residents during the pandemic and its downtown was weakened by remote work, but it's now enjoying a rejuvenation, with AI being the major driver.

Lauren Goldboss walks dogs for AI workers

Leading AI firms OpenAI and Anthropic â which have been hiring top engineering talent, often on eye-popping salaries â are based here. Anthropic, the company behind the Claude chatbot, last week listed on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

Thousands of other AI start-ups have also sprung up, external in San Francisco.

Signs of the boom are evident in soaring rents and house prices, falling office vacancies, and buses and billboards plastered with cryptic AI adverts.

But what if there is a significant downward correction in AI share prices and a resulting big fall in investment in the industry? The International Monetary Fund last month became the latest organization to warn, external that this was a possibility.

Concerns have since continued due to the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang, the former boss of AI firm Nvidia, based south of San Francisco, causing some wider AI stock falls. However, Nvidia remains highly profitable, and Wednesday of this week it reported that its latest quarterly profits had doubled from a year earlier to $65bn (Â£49bn).

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Wealthy AI workers send San Francisco house prices soaring

Follow the money: Who pays for the AI boom now?

What are the thoughts of AI workers in the city, and should San Francisco â no stranger to tech's boom-and-bust cycles â be preparing for a possible crash?

One AI worker employed in the city is convinced that a crash is coming. "I am certain that the house of cards will come downâ¦ and I'm sure it will affect me," says the man who asked that we didn't use his name.

He questions whether leading US AI companies can generate enough revenues to justify their enormous spending commitments as cheaper, increasingly capable AI models emerge, especially from China.

But he isn't losing sleep over it. "Why worry about things I can't control?"

Another San Francisco-based AI worker, who also asked to remain anonymous, says that a significant market correction is "totally possible".

Yet he adds that most people don't think of it as the core risk. Instead, he says that the bigger concern in his circles, and the one he shares, is the existential risk that AI could pose to humanity.

"In some weird way, a market correction being the worst-case outcome feels great."

If there is an AI crash, San Francisco could be hit far more than Silicon Valley which lies 40 miles to its south.

That's because the AI boom is very much centred on the city itself rather than the wider San Francisco Bay Area, which includes Silicon Valley.

One study, external found that 60% of all venture capital invested in US AI firms from 2023 to 2025 went to the companies located in San Francisco, dwarfing the significantly smaller percentage given to companies in Silicon Valley.

But some factors suggest that the impact of a crash in San Francisco may be softer than some fear.

For all the investment they have attracted, AI companies are employing relatively few workers in the city compared to previous tech booms. So there may not be that many jobs to lose in a downturn.

"Hiring isn't necessarily on the scale as we would have thought with the amount of venture capital and new company formation," says Jeff Bellisario, executive director of the Bay Area Council Economic Institute, a business-affiliated think tank.

Ted Egan, the chief economist for the City and County of San Francisco, adds that "because of AI, it is a good time for tech again in the city, but we are not seeing tonnes of overall tech job growth".

Despite the expansion of the AI sector, the city remains about 35,000 jobs shy of its pre-pandemic peak, estimates Enrico Moretti, an economics professor at the University of California, Berkeley.

Instead, much of the spending by AI firms in the US is going towards data centres and computing power, relatively little of which are located in the Bay Area. San Francisco might be the brains of the AI boom, but it isn't the brawn.

Nonetheless, a significant correction could still be "very painful" for San Francisco, warns Moretti. Less AI hiring and potential layoffs would ripple through the local service sector, likely to affect jobs in everything from restaurants, to retail, to personal services like dog walking.

"It is the spillover on service jobs that would actually be the main effect," he says.

Office vacancies could also rise, reducing downtown foot traffic, while rents and house prices could weaken.

Huge data centres are being built across the US to power AI

But AI isn't the only force driving San Francisco's recovery. Healthcare and tourism â which the city has been working to encourage, including by improving downtown safety and cleanliness â are also growing,, external which may help cushion against a possible AI downturn.

"Tourism is not fully back, but the growth has been healthy," says Egan, adding that it is a sign of confidence in San Francisco.

All told, Egan doesn't expect an AI crash to fundamentally alter San Francisco's economic trajectory.

"The city's economy has not been screaming during this AI boom, so I wouldn't expect it to grind to a halt if there was a slowdown in AI spending," he says. "I think of it primarily as a hiccup that we should expect, rather than a major course correction for the city's economy."

As to whether and how San Francisco's city government should prepare, there are limits to what it can do, note Bellisario and Moretti. But encouraging growth in sectors beyond technology and maintaining financial reserves could help.

For Goldboss, the dog-walking company owner, her philosophy is to "build while it is here". If the next tech bust comes â "always a reality in San Francisco" â she will try to adapt to what the market needs. "It is just the risk you kind of have to take," she says.

Cher: From chart-topping superstar to Oscar-winning actor

How the Hubble Space Telescope almost ended in catastrophe

#209. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#208. Commercial real estate activity falls 14% in October, as interest rates rise

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Interest rates moved higher in September, and that had a swift impact on commercial real estate deal-making. After settling in the low-to-mid-4% range, the yield on the 10-year Treasury moved above 5% and has been wavering around there with the potential to move even higher due to stubborn inflation.

As a result, commercial real estate activity dropped 14% in October from September, according to an index from LightBox, a commercial real estate data platform. Of the index's three components, commercial property listings fell 20% from September, environmental site assessments were down 1% and lender appraisals dropped 11%.

The appraisal measure is at its weakest level of the three and is now down 17% from a year ago. It shows that both lenders and borrowers are stepping back, unable to pencil deals and move from listing to closing.

"What makes this so interesting is that during tariffs, it didn't dip. During $100 oil earlier this year, it didn't dip. When we saw interest rates marching up the first half of this year, it didn't dip. In fact, it kind of increased, and now we've fallen off a map in October," said Manus Clancy, head of data strategy at LightBox. "It's like we've reached this straw that has broken the camel's back in terms of people saying, 'Okay, enough is enough. I can't keep putting money to work without some clarity on where interest rates are going.'"

Clancy said deals were happening earlier this year because investors had gotten more comfortable with where interest rates had settled. The office market was beginning to recover as more companies ordered workers back. Multifamily, which had seen a glut of new construction, was also starting to see vacancies fall and rents rise.

This takes the market back to 2023, when deal-making froze up because interest rates had shot up sharply from very low, pandemic-induced levels. As property values repriced, the gap between what the sellers wanted and what the buyers were willing to pay widened. That is what is happening now.

"The wheels of progress just completely stop," said Clancy.

Whether they are refinancing a loan or getting a new loan to purchase a property, investors, developers and property owners are now deciding to wait to see if interest rates come down at all. While the Federal Reserve is expected to raise its lending rate further, an end to the war with Iran could bring Treasury yields down. Especially for refinances, borrowers would be out millions of dollars at these higher rates. On the buy side, deals simply don't work anymore at higher rates.

"I think it is disorienting as people try to rebase values, and in the midst of transactions, you're seeing value retrades at the 11th hour, deals getting pulled, and volume slowing down yet again in the market," said Ryan Cotton, head of real estate at Bain Capital in a recent Property Play podcast. "It's definitely a confusing and complicated time to be a real estate investor."

Higher interest rates were the leading factor for the slowdown in October, but LightBox cited another contributing factor. Lending remains open, but sentiment has weakened. It points to the Mortgage Bankers Association cutting its 2027 growth forecast for commercial mortgage lending from about 8% to 1%. In addition, the Commercial Real Estate Finance Council, or CREFC, Sentiment Index fell 17.5% in the third quarter of this year to a three-year low.

"People are lending for construction. They're lending for office conversion. They're lending for new purchases," noted Clancy. "We look at this index as kind of the tip of the spear. That if something's going to go sideways, this is the first indicator that people are stopping the ordering of appraisals, and we think that this portends that the next quarter will show a lot of decline in bank lending on commercial real estate."

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#206. UK to explore even closer ties with EU after agreeing deal, says Burnham

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen, Prime Minister Andy Burnham and European Council President Antonio Costa join hands at the end of a press conference during the EU-UK summit in Brussels

Andy Burnham said the UK will discuss possible options for even closer ties with the EU, after agreeing a deal covering British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

The prime minister said it was a "good day" for Britain and Europe, adding the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

European Council President AntÃ³nio Costa said the EU remains "open to have the closest possible relationship" with the UK.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal "isn't fair" - and Reform's Nigel Farage called it a "betrayal of the British people".

Legislation to implement the deal is expected to be introduced in the UK Parliament next week.

Burnham arrived in Brussels to finalise the latest stage of the government's post-Brexit "reset" of relations with the EU, which originally began under former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer.

The agreements announced on Friday included scrapping most of the checks on British food exports, in return for the UK following some EU regulations, from 2028.

The UK government believes the changes will cut costs for businesses and could bring prices down for shoppers, while critics argue the change will make the UK a "rule taker" from Brussels rather than a rule maker.

Watch: What the new UK-EU deal could mean for European relations

The new youth experience scheme will allow up to 100,000 18 to 31 year olds from the EU to travel, work and study in the UK for up to three years. The same number of young Britons will be allowed into Europe.

The two sides want to ensure that opportunities under the annual scheme will be available to young people by no later than January 2030.

A further six months is available to discuss a deal on tuition fees, which could prove to be a potential stumbling block.

There have been calls from the European side for fees to be reduced for EU students at British universities. The joint statement says any agreement would also be for UK students in the EU.

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she is "confident" a solution will be found, with Burnham adding: "Me too."

On immigration, the UK and EU have committed to working closely to tackle migrant smuggling and human trafficking. They also expect their law enforcement agencies to share more data.

Burnham said "significant progress" has been made to help "disrupt the gangs who are making those dangerous crossings across the English Channel".

The UK-EU statement also said talks on linking the EU and UK emissions trading systems have concluded and they aim to apply the agreement from 2028.

Watch: Will the UK-EU summit deal boost the economy?

Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

He added: "Beyond that we do need to consider what is the right long-term relationship. Incremental steps only take us so far."

The prime minister said his government will publish a 10-year plan next month, adding it will set out its ambitions for reindustrialisation, growth in "all parts of Britain" and opportunities for young people.

Burnham said these ambitions are "connected to our long-term relationship with our nearest partner".

He said over the coming months before the next UK-EU summit both sides will "seek to conduct an informal brainstorming process" to look at what those options are.

Burnham added: "What we are announcing today is probably the limit of what we can achieve in this Parliament, given what needs to be done to implement it.

"But we want to inform the Parliament after this one, so that decisions can be taken about that long-term relationship."

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The prime minister said the "British people will be the boss of what happens next", with the next election needing to be held by August 2029.

Von der Leyen, who said the summit was a "turning point" for the EU-UK relationship, said "we are open for brainstorming".

Badenoch said the deal was not fair because UK farmers would now have to follow rules made by Brussels and more young people will come to the UK to "compete with our young people who are struggling to get jobs".

Badenoch was also asked about the Treasury's analysis, which Burnham said suggested the deal "will add up to 0.5%" to UK gross domestic product and he described it as a "Â£17bn boost to the British economy".

She claimed Burnham has "plucked that figure out of the air" and "the only modelling I've seen that even talks about that figure says it's actually 0.0%".

Farage said Friday was a "very good day for the EU", adding: "A good day to try and humiliate the one member state that actually had the courage to say, 'We don't need to be part of you, we can govern our own country'."

In a social media post, Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, added: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud. Only Reform will stop the endless betrayal of the British people."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

#204. MP criticises Guernsey and Jersey company ownership register plans

MP Sir Andrew Mitchell has campaigned for publicly accessible registers of who owns companies in British Overseas Territories and Crown Dependencies

Plans to improve transparency over company ownership in Guernsey and Jersey have come under criticism from a Westminster MP, who says they "do not go nearly far enough".

The comments come after Guernsey, external and Jersey, external set out plans to allow approved applicants access to information about who owns or controls companies when investigating suspected financial crime.

Sir Andrew Mitchell, who has campaigned for greater scrutiny of company ownership, said there were "immediate indications" the proposals would fall short.

He said limits on access to the information could mean "national security threats may remain entirely invisible to investigators".

The plans follow a joint commitment made by Guernsey, Jersey and the Isle of Man in December 2023 to increase transparency around company ownership and help tackle money laundering, terrorist financing and other financial crime.

Currently, information about who ultimately owns or controls companies is generally only available to law enforcement agencies, regulators and certain regulated businesses carrying out anti-money laundering checks.

The new proposals would allow journalists, academics and other applicants with a legitimate interest to apply for access.

Sir Andrew said investigators could face a "catch-22" because they may need access to the register to prove wrongdoing in the first place.

He also said he understood applicants would have to show they had been unable to find the information elsewhere, arguing: "This entirely defeats the object of a register."

Jersey's consultation response says applicants would be expected to show that "all reasonable alternative sources have been exhausted" before access was granted.

Guernsey's published response does not explicitly set out the same requirement.

He said: "Access should be open to those from other jurisdictions, for example those in the global south from whom much of the world's dirty money was stolen in the first place."

Sir Andrew also questioned whether users would have to pay to access the information, saying: "This information should be free at the point of access."

He said the proposals should eventually lead to fully public registers.

Both the governments of Guernsey and Jersey rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and said they continue to meet international standards in tackling financial crime.

A Government of Jersey spokesperson said the concerns raised by him "do not accurately reflect what is in Jersey's consultation response paper".

They said the policy was intended to help Jersey meet its international commitments while maintaining "a fair, proportionate, and effective transparency framework".

They added: "Jersey continues to meet international standards for transparency and countering money laundering, terrorist financing and proliferation financing."

Deputy Andrew Niles, from Guernsey's Policy & Resources Committee, rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and pointed to a positive 2025 assessment of the island's efforts to tackle financial crime, external.

He said: "Guernsey does not accept the suggestion that our proposals fall short of the commitments we've made on beneficial ownership transparency or that the island does not meet international standards on beneficial ownership transparency."

"The proposal is based on a tried and tested model adopted in the EU," he added.

#203. Kushner-backed OakNorth secures U.S. bank charter as global digital banks push into America

The U.K.'s OakNorth has secured a federal bank charter, saying it is the first foreign digital bank to do so, as international competitors vie for U.S. market share.

The charter clears OakNorth, which is backed by Jared Kushner's private equity firm, to acquire Michigan-based Community Unity Bank, subject to an $80 million capital injection, according to approval documents issued by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency on Nov. 19.

The deal, which is expected to close by the end of 2026, would allow OakNorth to provide loans, take deposits, and offer other banking services across all 50 states.

Kushner's private equity firm, Affinity Partners, took an 8% stake in OakNorth in 2025, with backing from major Gulf investors, including the sovereign wealth funds of Saudi Arabia and Qatar. SoftBank is also an OakNorth investor.

The bank reported $295 million in pretax profit for 2025 in its annual report, up from $280 million the previous year and close to three times the $100 million in profits five years prior.

It said principal losses on $20 billion in credit facilities issued over the past 10 years totaled £9 million, or less than 0.05%. Its 1.4% return on assets placed it among the top five banks in Western Europe and in the top 1% of commercial banks globally, according to The Banker's rankings.

"I would say that the level of animal spirits in the U.S. is always higher, right? So the momentum of business and the optimism is always a step up," OakNorth CEO Rishi Khosla told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Monday.

He said U.S. economic growth, investment in artificial intelligence and infrastructure spending would create opportunities for the bank.

OakNorth has focused on lending to lower mid-market businesses rather than competing for consumer customers in a crowded U.S. market.

The lender targets businesses with annual revenues of $5 million to $100 million with loans worth $5 million to $50 million. The bank entered the U.S. market in 2023 and has lent about $3.5 billion to U.S. businesses to date. The U.S. accounted for 40% of its $12.4 billion in new loans in 2025.

"These are really growth companies which are scaling up, and given that focus, you really haven't had an institution in the last decades that focus on that customer segment, and that's opened up the opportunity for us," Khosla said.

He cited commercial real estate lending as a particularly strong growth opportunity in the U.S.

Securing the charter is notoriously difficult, with many regulatory hurdles. It took OakNorth 20 months, a restructure of the bank's corporate ownership, and relocating its holding company from the island of Jersey to the U.K. to meet Federal Reserve requirements.

Other international fintechs seeking a foothold in the U.S. have struggled to gain traction. Monzo and Revolut withdrew their initial U.S. applications for a full banking charter between 2021 and 2023 after regulatory pushback.

Monzo closed its U.S. operations in April just four years after it launched, amid a crowded consumer market. "We have made a strategic decision to focus on scaling in our home market and Europe and to step away from the U.S.," the company said.

But the regulatory landscape has since shifted. Revolut and Brazil's Nubank have received conditional approval for U.S. banking charters from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Dutch digital bank Bunq reapplied after withdrawing its 2023 application, but its second application was denied in August.

Another U.K. digital lender, Starling, is also seeking U.S. growth. It sells its Engine banking software to mid-tier U.S. lenders and is looking to acquire a domestic bank to serve business clients.

#200. Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

The prime minister made a lot of the UK's new deal with the EU cutting costs for business and consumers during tough times.

In another world, Andy Burnham would have loved for the nation's supermarkets to clearly announce that they could cut food prices as a result of this latest agreement on food exports.

And while grocers have very much welcomed Friday's announcement, they have notably not committed to price cuts as a direct result of this move.

The managing director of M&S, Alex Freudmann, called the deal a "major step forward", saying it would remove bureaucracy. Tesco chief executive Ken Murphy added it was "good news for British farmers and producers".

Freudmann highlighted some of hoops that were hurting UK-EU trade, using the example that "getting our spaghetti Bolognese from Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet's signature and even the Latin name of a cow".

Such measures will no longer need to be taken once this deal kicks in. Good news for business exports then, but will that lead to lower shopping bills here in the UK?

This deal will matter for food exporters - it may not have a clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders say there are too many moving parts in the world economy, especially given the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

It is more a hope than will at this stage. The industry and government hope that the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers.

This is not the first time Labour has sought to reduce the weekly shop costs. Sir Keir Starmer's administration floated the idea that supermarkets might even offer some form of branded discounts, a suggestion that was met with collective bemusement by those in the industry.

However, there will be less paperwork, checks and red tape on food imports too, but they were already lightly enforced.

No longer will hundreds of pages of documents be required. No longer will perishable goods lose valuable hours or days at border checks. No longer will the incorrect colour of ink or Latin name for a meat disallow a shipment of British exports.

Some, though not all of the 24% hit since 2019 to the real value of agrifood exports, could be regained by a bonfire of this red tape.

In particular, smaller exporters of food will be able to return to the large EU market.

But the extra benefit here is unclear, and there could be extra checks and costs required instead on non-EU food imports.

The government's own assessment is that the economy will gain Â£5bn through this, or 0.14% by 2040.

Critics point out that this benefit does not include the quid pro quo - having to align with EU laws. Farmers have pointed to compliance with pesticides and fungicide regulation.

The classic example of the benefits of post-Brexit autonomy has been the development of precision-bred foodstuffs, such as Vitamin D-enhanced tomatoes.

The producer of this technology has, however, backed the deal after the government negotiated an "exception period" for ongoing UK sale.

Professor Cristobal Uauy of the John Innes Centre said that "aligning with the EU market will bring our science to a greater number of people".

Bringing all the agreements on food, emissions and the youth experience together, the economy is forecast to get an overall boost of 0.5%, or Â£17bn a year.

A modest boost, but high by the standards of past post-Brexit trade deals, for example, with Australia, India or Japan and the Gulf and the trans-pacific deal.

This UK-EU pact actually matches all of those agreements combined for expected growth measured by the Treasury's own assessments.

The biggest single boost to the overall economy would come from more European young workers. However, the economy benefit of this part of the deal per person reduces to zero, according to the government's own calculations.

Burham also said he hoped a planned electricity deal with the EU would cut bills, but that has not been done yet, and requires a contribution into the bloc's budget for poorer regions.

The electricity deal could help lower bills, but that contains a clear trade-off.

There are still some details to be revealed in the full legal texts of these documents.

The bigger point is whether this logic of gaining free-flowing access for food, in exchange for giving up regulatory freedom, will now apply elsewhere.

Is food now a blueprint for future negotiations? It had been the Starmer-Rachel Reeves strategy to spread this model to other sectors such as cars, and chemicals.

The big takeaway from today was that both the PM and his counterparts publicly acknowledged the end of the incremental piecemeal approach to aligning with the single market.

The question is whether the PM thinks what goes for food applies across the whole economy. Most economists predict that could lead to much bigger economic gains, but it will also be a much bigger constitutional battle.

#199. Nvidia’s stock drops as these major questions loom over Jensen Huang’s return

Huang’s reinstatement as CEO doesn’t fix regulatory issues or the investigation into his relationship with a former Megaspeed executive.

→ Baca artikel asli

#198. Six key questions raised by Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang made a dramatic return as Nvidia CEO on Monday, less than a week after board Chairman Harvey Jones said directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back.

Nvidia shares surged as much as 36% during the session and closed 31% higher.

The reversal followed a steep decline in Nvidia shares, which closed at $125.53 on Friday even after the company reported strong third-quarter results. A petition calling for Huang's return had drawn more than 200,000 signatures, according to its organizers.

The rally spread across the broader market, with the Nasdaq Composite gaining 6.5% and the S&P 500 rising 4.7%, each posting its strongest session since April 2025.

Nvidia's board said it had received input from senior U.S. government officials and that its investigation into Huang's conduct would continue.

Still, the board's reversal, President Donald Trump's administration's role and continued scrutiny of Nvidia's China business leave major questions for both investors and lawmakers.

Sen. Jack Reed, a member of the Senate Banking Committee, urged Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Monday to appear before the committee to discuss the case.

Reed did not lay out a specific list of questions for the two officials, but his statement cited reporting that Trump had been briefed by intelligence officials led by then-National Security Adviser Marco Rubio before intervening in support of Huang's return.

Reed's call adds to mounting pressure on both Nvidia and the Trump administration to explain what they knew and when.

Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., vice ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said Huang’s reinstatement could broaden congressional scrutiny rather than bring it to a close.

“I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query,” Amo told CNBC, adding that lawmakers should also examine administration officials involved in decisions affecting Nvidia and chip sales to China.

Amo said he expects the planned closed-door interview with Huang on Dec. 1 to proceed and that Congress would continue seeking answers both privately and publicly in the coming months.

“He should start purchasing his plane tickets to Washington,” he said. “It is abundantly clear that this is ripe for questioning by Congress.”

On Nvidia's third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 17, Jones said the board had accepted Huang's resignation and did not intend to reinstate him.

Less than a week later, the board reversed course, saying Monday that it had unanimously voted to bring Huang back after discussions with him and input from senior U.S. government officials.

Trump said he had spoken with board members and that his administration provided assurances based on the information available to it. But the board has not publicly detailed what it was told.

"The board decided to do what we wanted to do," Trump told CNBC, adding that he believed his intervention had been important.

"I've been speaking to the board directly and indirectly, and I felt very strongly about it," he said.

Trump also called into an Nvidia board meeting to argue for Huang's return, Semafor reported, citing people familiar with the matter.

Reed has called on Huang to disclose his communications with Trump and on Nvidia to release a transcript of the board's call with the president.

Lawmakers are still seeking answers about the alleged smuggling of Nvidia products to China, their possible diversion for military use and possible advancement of Chinese AI capabilities.

The Senate Banking Committee has scheduled Huang to testify on U.S. export controls on Dec. 3. House lawmakers have also confirmed closed-door interviews with Huang on Dec. 1 and Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter on Dec. 2.

"The Nvidia board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," Reps. John Moolenaar, Rick Crawford and Brian Mast said in a joint statement.

Their inquiry may also examine what Nvidia knew about the customers and resellers involved, as well as what controls the company had in place.

Other lawmakers have also sought to preserve the paper trail. Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Chris Coons had asked senior administration officials on Nov. 11 to retain records related to Nvidia exports of advanced AI chips to China and associated national security concerns.

Nvidia has promised to hire a chief compliance officer and establish a committee overseeing national security and export controls. The board's investigation into Huang's conduct also remains underway.

Josh Gilbert, eToro's lead analyst for APAC and the Middle East, said investors will be watching whether those new safeguards carry meaningful authority.

Gil Luria, head of technology research at D.A. Davidson, said Nvidia has a limited window to prepare for the possibility that a future president may be less supportive of Huang.

With Trump's term ending in two years, Luria argued that Nvidia needs a clearer plan for who could take over if Huang were forced to leave again.

Gilbert pointed to Nvidia's roughly 75% revenue-growth forecast for the next financial year as evidence of strong near-term demand. The longer-term question, he said, is whether customer orders and artificial intelligence investment can sustain that pace.

Luria told CNBC that concerns about the broader AI trade remain, including the cost of financing data centers, potential overbuilding by neocloud companies and obstacles to building new facilities.

Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon said worries about whether AI spending is achievable and sustainable were not caused solely by Huang's departure. UBS, meanwhile, flagged Nvidia's roughly $390 billion in supply commitments and the need to keep demand flowing through its ecosystem.

Gilbert said part of Monday's rally reflected investors recovering earlier losses rather than pricing in new growth. Bernstein restored its "Outperform" rating and kept its $200 price target, while UBS maintained its $220 target.

"For the share price, it comes down to whether Nvidia can keep delivering as it has done over recent years," said Gilbert, adding that Huang's return could ease some concerns about Nvidia's longer-term outlook.

"The real worry has been what happens beyond next year. Having Huang back at the helm goes a long way to easing those concerns, because he's the one who sits down with the biggest customers and gets those multi-year orders over the line," he said.

— CNBC's David Sucherman, Justin Papp and Eko Prasetyo contributed to the story

#194. Investigators make progress in hunt for cause of FAA ground stop

Engineers and analysts searching for the root cause of the technical issues behind Friday's nationwide ground stop by the Federal Aviation Administration have been able to replicate the situation that was overwhelming parts of the air traffic system, people familiar with the investigation told CNBC.

While replicating the issue in a lab in Florida is progress, those investigating the matter have yet to determine exactly why two routers in question failed, according to the people, who requested anonymity to talk about an ongoing situation. Finding the root cause is critical to the FAA determining if there may be an issue with other routers that form the backbone of communications for the national air transportation network.

The ground stop lasted for more than four hours Friday, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy and FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford have said there is no indication the routers failed due to a cyberattack.

The incident in question happened shortly after L3Harris Technologies, the primary contractor supporting the FAA telecommunications network, installed a new router at a facility in El Dorado, Arkansas.

L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

Investigators said within eight minutes of its installation, the router began to transmit too much data within the FAA network.

That surge of data, 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds, destabilized aircraft communications and radar feeds, Duffy said.

Since the ground stop, Duffy has increased the amount of money he wants to upgrade the national air traffic system. After originally asking for $30 billion to build a digital network that will incorporate artificial intelligence, Duffy has now asked Congress for $50 billion.

#193. Drugmaker Insmed shares rally 14% after Sanofi agrees to buy it in pharma’s biggest deal in three years

Insmed shares rallied 14% on Tuesday after Sanofi announced it would buy the smaller drugmaker for $41 billion, marking the largest pharma deal since 2023.

The proposed acquisition includes Insmed's portfolio of two commercialized respiratory drugs as well as its pipeline of experimental candidates. Sanofi will pay $175 per share in cash.

It's a big bet by new Sanofi CEO Belen Garijo, who took up the role in May to steer the company through its upcoming patent cliff, when its blockbuster immunology drug Dupixent will face generic competition as patents expire.

If closed, the transaction will be the biggest one the industry has seen since Pfizer bought Seagen for $43 billion in December 2023.

Insmed shares closed up 14.1% at $170.15 on Tuesday, while Sanofi stock closed up 10.9% in Paris.

Barclays analysts called the deal a "breath of fresh air," significantly improving Sanofi's Dupixent patent expiry outlook by adding Insmed's established respiratory assets Brinsupri and Arikayce, plus a late-stage pipeline offering further potential upside.

The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2027 and be accretive to earnings by the end of 2028, Sanofi said.

Like much of the industry, Sanofi faces a revenue drop toward the end of the decade as top-selling drugs lose exclusivity and open up for generic drugmakers to compete.

Global biopharma has experienced a sharp uptick in deals in 2026, as the sector is driven by looming patent cliffs, newly buoyant public markets and a race to beef up pipelines. Following a post-pandemic trough in 2022, M&A activity surged in 2025 and has accelerated into 2026.

While companies develop new medicines in-house, acquiring them is often faster and has become the go-to strategy for large biopharma companies with strong cash flows and financial firepower. Investors are also calling for sustainable revenue growth as major drugs lose exclusivity.

"Sanofi could offer a far more attractive investment opportunity given the significantly improved growth outlook," Barclays said in a note early Tuesday.

Formerly CEO at Merck KGaA, Garijo is facing pressure to find Sanofi's next growth driver. Her predecessor Paul Hudson stepped down earlier this year after the board chose not to renew his mandate, following a six-year reign during which his costly emphasis on R&D failed to deliver speedy results.

The Insmed deal is a good strategic fit that will help soften the blow from Dupixent's expected 2031–32 loss of exclusivity, said Jefferies analysts.

Insmed's medicines Brinsupri and Arikayce could add significant long-term revenue, with forecasts of $6.8 billion and $1.3 billion, respectively, in 2035, the analysts said. They see further potential upside if a settlement delays Dupixent's patent cliff, or if Brinsupri is included in Sanofi's alliance with Regeneron and positioned alongside Dupixent in respiratory diseases.

"The deal does not fix Sanofi's R&D/pipeline questions, but it buys time," the analysts said, adding that one potential wrinkle is future competition from AstraZeneca's tozorakimab, which is being studied for treatment in bronchiectasis, a chronic lung condition.

"Insmed builds on our already successful presence in respiratory diseases through its highly differentiated portfolio, strong science and proven commercial success," CEO Garijo said in a statement.

Sanofi shares fell 2.9%, and Insmed rose 24% on Nov. 19 after Bloomberg reported that a deal was underway.

#192. Italian jeweller and businessman Paolo Bulgari dies aged 89

Italian jeweller Paolo Bulgari, who helped turn his family's business into a global luxury brand, has died in Rome aged age 89.

His family announced the news, remembering him as "the greatest jeweller of our time", according to Italian media.

Paolo Bulgari was Chairman of the Bvlgari Group since 1984 and its jewellery pieces have adorned Hollywood stars and royalty including Diana, Princess of Wales.

The company Bvlgari posted an online tribute to the "true creative soul", whose work would "continue to inspire generations to come".

The firm, Bulgari, was set up by his grandfather, Greek silversmith Sotirios Voulgaris, in Rome in the late 19th century - who later adapted his name to Bulgari in Italian.

Originally from Paramythia, a mountain village in the Epirus region of northwestern Greece renowned for its silver jewellery, Voulgaris fled Ottoman rule and settled in Naples before opening a shop in Rome.

Paolo Bulgari joined the company shortly after leaving school, becoming an expert in gemstones - eventually leading the business and overseeing international growth.

The Bulgari family favoured bold gemstones, rich yellow gold, generous proportions and unexpected colour combinations to set them apart from the delicate asethetic associated with Parisian jewellery houses.

The business broke into luxury markets and diversified beyond jewellery into watches, perfumes, leather goods and hotels.

"Paolo Bulgari began collaborating with his father in 1960, specialising in the knowledge of precious stones, of which he was one of the world's leading experts, and refining the sensibility and aesthetic sense that are at the root of the Bvlgari style," the Bvlgari company posted on social media., external

"He poured his heart into his craft and into nurturing the talents of young designers, leaving an indelible mark on the world of jewellery.

"Thank you, Mr. Paolo, your legacy as a brilliant beacon of creative inspiration will continue to inspire generations to come."

Paolo Bulgari alongside David Bowie and his wife Iman in September 1991 in Versailles, France.

The post included a quote from Paolo Bulgari: "Everything can be a source of inspiration. There are no rules in creativity. Never."

The designer brand's celebrity clients have included Elizabeth Taylor, Audrey Hepburn, Marilyn Monroe, Ingrid Bergman, Sophie Loren, Anne Hathaway, Zendaya and Rihanna.

Actor Zendaya wearing a Bvlgari necklace at the 2025 Golden Globe awards.

In 2011 the family sold its controlling stake to the French luxury group LVMH, in a deal worth about 3.7 billion euros.

Chairman and CEO of LVMH, Bernard Arnault - who is one of the world's richest people - offered his condolences and also paid tribute to Paolo Bulgari.

"Throughout his life, he was a key architect of the brand's success, while simultaneously upholding and embodying the Italian spirit and the deeply Roman heritage of our exceptional jewellery Maison," he said in a statement to the BBC.

In 2019, Paolo Bulgari and his wife established the Paolo Bulgari Foundation to support young people through schooling and job training in disadvantaged neighbourhoods on the outskirts of Rome.

The funeral will take place in Rome on Monday, 23 November in the Basilica of Santa Croce in Gerusalemme.

#190. Op-ed: Trump’s Nvidia board intervention put a price on national security. Our adversaries will take note

To learn more about the CNBC CFO Council, visit cnbccouncils.com/cfo

President Donald J. Trump has accomplished something extraordinary. He directly inserted himself into the boardroom of one of America's most strategically important companies, Nvidia, helping to reverse a corporate leadership decision, and personally vouched for restored CEO Jensen Huang as having a clean bill of health regarding the national security investigations into his business activities with China.

On Monday, after Nvidia's board reinstated Huang following his resignation amid a counterintelligence controversy and allegations that Alice Huang, with whom he had an affair, is under suspicion of being a Chinese intelligence asset, Wall Street celebrated with a historic market bounce. But the market's enthusiasm should not obscure a far more consequential question circulating in Washington, D.C.: Does American national security have a price?

What happened at Nvidia raises the frightening possibility that it does, and it fits a recent pattern that is exactly what geopolitical rivals around the world want to see. Consider the economic pressures surrounding the Iran war. Disruptions to energy markets, shipping, supply chains, and consumer prices have complicated American strategic decision-making. These are notably different circumstances, but they raise the same troubling question about the costs of defending American national security interests, and when they become economically unpalatable to those in the Trump administration.

Beijing, Tehran, and Moscow are watching — as are our allies and partners — and they all may agree on one thing: America has an Achilles' heel that military and technological superiority cannot overcome. It is possible to impose sufficient economic pain to alter Washington's strategic calculation with friends and foes alike. Bleed American capital markets, disrupt commerce, squeeze consumers, and eventually Washington may look for an exit.

Despite the narrative that the president is weaving, there is a fundamental difference between responsibly weighing economic consequences and allowing adversaries to conclude that they can impose an economic veto over U.S. national security policy. Trump told CNBC in an extensive interview on Monday morning that when it comes to national security, "losing the brain power of somebody like Jensen ... would be a far bigger threat than anything we're talking about."

"The bigger impact was the long term. The bigger impact was you know he's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about, including me, that nobody else understands," Trump said. "This was a big loss. This was a tremendous loss. And you saw that it affected the market. It affected even, I think it even affected the perception of our country in the world," the president said.

And that's true, but not only in the way Trump thinks it did.

This is not about whether Huang is a brilliant executive or whether Nvidia is enormously important to American competitiveness. Both are true. The question that America, particularly its government, must ask is whether Huang's economic importance justifies subordinating corporate governance, interfering with counterintelligence procedures, and stymying national security oversight.

Trump characterized the Justice Department's assessment as having exonerated Huang. Yet Nvidia's board reinstated him while its own review remained open.

The allegations involving reported contacts with Chinese intelligence assets do not establish legal wrongdoing. However, presidential personal assurances are not substitutes for independent counterintelligence assessments and investigations or congressional oversight. Monday's rising stock price might only measure what shareholders believe Huang at the helm of Nvidia is worth, while discounting or ignoring the unresolved counterintelligence risk that he may still pose to Nvidia and the entire artificial intelligence ecosystem.

The Trump administration, Silicon Valley, and Wall Street may believe the matter is closed.

"That thing's like a rocket ship going in the wrong direction. Now it's a rocket ship going in the right direction, and it's taking the whole market," Trump told CNBC about Nvidia's 31.5% Monday gains.

One glaring vulnerability still on the horizon is what Washington does not know. If Beijing possesses additional damaging information, it controls whether, when, and how that information might be weaponized in an ongoing and now reignited strategic competition between the U.S. and China. Beijing retains the capacity to reopen this matter at a time of its choosing. Chinese intelligence could drip-feed disclosures, compel repeated explanations from Huang and the president, and transform Monday's declaration of resolution of this matter into a prolonged national security vulnerability.

There is also a striking irony here. Some pundits are comparing Trump's intervention to China's state-controlled market. To be sure, the Chinese government has a very heavy hand in its economy, but one thing the Chinese Communist Party will never do is put an individual ahead of national security and political authority despite the economic impact that this person or company is perceived to have. Xi Jinping and China's Communist Party have demonstrated a willingness to make almost the opposite calculation about national security and economic growth. Under Xi, national and political security generally takes precedence over the interests of individual companies and brilliant executives.

Consider Jack Ma. In 2020, Beijing halted Ant Group's planned $37 billion initial public offering and subsequently imposed sweeping regulatory restrictions on Ma's business empire. Beijing accepted substantial economic consequences to reinforce national security and political authority. This is not an argument for importing China's authoritarian model into American corporate governance. But the strategic asymmetry is noteworthy to revisit in this latest comparative context. Beijing has demonstrated that even its most celebrated entrepreneurs are not indispensable to the party and the nation. Washington risks signaling that some of its entrepreneurs are in fact indispensable to the state, which ironically potentially makes them even bigger intelligence targets for foreign adversaries.

As an economic matter, the commercial realities of Nvidia's China business make this situation even more perplexing. China, including Hong Kong, accounted for just 9.1% of Nvidia's reported global revenue in fiscal 2026, down from 19.2% the previous year. Its latest fourth-quarter guidance assumes no Chinese data center compute revenue. Nvidia did change the way it reports geographic sales, to customer headquarter address, in late 2025. But anyone familiar with Chinese strategic documents, including the 15th Five-Year Plan, should understand that Beijing has no intention of becoming permanently "addicted" to American chips. Its objective is technological self-reliance, not expanding Nvidia's market share and by extension its dependency on them.

Whatever the exact figure, I cannot think of a number that is worth selling out U.S. national security over, but it certainly isn't  9.1%, which is also a number that will continue to shrink. Furthermore, if the AI ecosystem is critical to our economy and our national security, then selling advanced chips to China or loosening export controls is the opposite of what we should be doing because the chips are used to juice the compute of Chinese LLMs that compete with ours, and to increase the effectiveness and lethality of Chinese weapons systems. The questionable sales of Nvidia chips in Asia, which are at the heart of this story, support neither our economy nor our national security. They undermine them both.

There is also an extraordinary corporate governance precedent that extends well beyond Nvidia and Huang, one that is escalating and growing more bold at each turn. While Trump has intervened in the markets before, in cases including U.S. Steel and Intel, he has never gone to this level, making the argument that a company is so critical to the national security of the nation that at any point its decision making could require executive branch intervention and specific direction.

Based on the president's rationale, what should the boards of key national security relevant companies like Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Palantir, Anduril, SpaceX, or even Rio Tinto — whose jointly owned Resolution Copper project in Arizona could eventually supply up to 25 percent of America's copper demand — expect?  Does their strategic importance justify presidential intervention in executive appointments, succession planning, corporate strategy, or commercial relationships? A precedent established by one president becomes an idea, option, and instrument available to future presidents, including those with fundamentally different political preferences.

All of this brings us to Congress. If you take Trump at his word, that Nvidia's strategic importance justifies extraordinary executive branch intervention, it also justifies extraordinary legislative intervention and oversight. Article I grants Congress authority to regulate foreign commerce. Congress established the statutory framework governing executive export-control authority through legislation, including the Export Control Reform Act of 2018. Those authorities were delegated, not surrendered. The president may have driven this Nvidia CEO decision by telephone. Congress possesses something more enduring: the authority to establish the rules through legislation. The Senate Banking Committee's scheduled December 3 hearing on export controls provides an opportunity to begin.

Congress should do much more than demand classified briefings on the counterintelligence concerns.  Lawmakers should pass, with veto-proof majorities, requirements for stronger board-level counterintelligence responsibilities, mandatory defensive briefings to vulnerable tech executives and innovation sector leadership, much tighter export-license oversight with enforcement mechanisms that have teeth, and enforceable reporting requirements. Legislative proposals including the AI Overwatch Act, Match Act, and Remote Access Security Act must be passed as soon as possible. Nvidia and its leadership may warrant considerably tighter legal and regulatory scrutiny given its importance as the fulcrum of the AI ecosystem.

It is important for Congress to remember that the legislative branch is a co-equal branch of government, not a rubber stamp or talk shop providing us with commentary about the outcome of executive branch decisions. They have an active and constitutional role to play in our democracy. They need to get it together and get to work. If Nvidia is important enough for the president to enter its boardroom, it is important enough for Congress to exercise its constitutional authority over the rules governing this entire process.

—By Dewardic McNeal, Managing Director and Senior Policy Analyst at Longview Global, and a CNBC Contributor

#189. Can AI really help spot Black Friday deals? We put it to the test

Whether it's scrolling through endless choice, spotting fake reviews, or working out if a "deal" is a deal at all, Black Friday sums up a lot of online woes.

Retailers promise bargains - but the sheer number of offers can leave us feeling no closer to finding the best deal.

It's an area where generative AI products, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini, have promised to help.

But are they any good at finding Black Friday deals? We had a go ourselves a week ahead of the big day.

Figures suggest relatively few UK shoppers currently go to AI tools for help.

A recent YouGov survey of 19 countries found the UK ranked second-last in the proportion of people who said they had used AI to help them shop.

Crucially, 36% of respondents who said they would be shopping around Black Friday didn't plan to use an AI tool at all.

But the survey suggested a growing number of people were considering it - and tech firms have released features to capitalise on this.

Amazon launched Alexa for Shopping for UK users in November and OpenAI launched a Shopping Research tool on ChatGPT days before last year's Black Friday.

In the US, Google recently launched Universal Cart, with AI-powered features such as price tracking and identification of relevant promo codes.

But there has been criticism that fact-checking AI ends up taking more time, while some brands are working to try to make sure their products show up in chatbot answers.

We asked four models to find a 55-inch TV deal, using the free versions of many of the most popular tools.

While this was not a rigorous scientific test, the experience we had produced mixed results.

OpenAI's ChatGPT came up with four options.

Its top pick was a Hisense TV on Chinese retailer Joybuy's website, on sale for Â£499 down from Â£649.

The next recommendation was a 55-inch TCL-branded TV, but a sidebar showed prices for the 50-inch version, from Â£379 to Â£814.

Option three was the same Hisense TV as before, but for full price at Â£649 from John Lewis and Richer Sounds.

This was the "best safer purchase" according to ChatGPT, as the sites offered longer guarantee periods.

But clicking on the links did not go well. Both retailers said the TV was no longer listed online.

The fourth option was equally fruitless - another TCL TV, at full price, Â£150 over our budget and not available online.

At the bottom, ChatGPT served one last offer - "one OLED option to compare" - for a Â£1,100 LG TV that was marked as "Ad".

Claude produced the shortest answer of all the models we tested, and unlike the others, it did not give much detail on the specifications of each model.

Instead, it seemed to base its results on a small number of reviews published online.

It recommended the same two TCL TVs as ChatGPT.

But when we clicked on the links, they sent us to two dead pages on the Richer Sounds website.

Some other links led to year-old round-ups of Black Friday 2025 deals.

Claude was not very enthusiastic about its other two recommendations due to a lack of independent reviews - calling one "a decent option" but saying it "wouldn't pick" the other.

It also listed two Samsung TVs, but it told us not to buy them, with one "over budget and not clearly better than the TCLs".

We were left wondering why it even mentioned them in the first place.

Different AI model, same TV manufacturers - albeit different Hisense and TCL models to the previous chatbots' recommendations.

Gemini generated a table comparing four different models, but offered no links to the stores it named, so we searched for them ourselves.

The prices for one TCL TV were correct at Â£649, but it was sold out at one of the named retailers.

It said the other TCL model was available from Argos and Currys for Â£539 - correct for Argos, but when we checked Currys, it was Â£60 more expensive.

Again, it included two Samsung models to avoid due to lower screen specs.

Alexa came back with four TVs, from its own Amazon Ember at Â£532 to a Â£699 Samsung.

But its top recommendation was to go for another Samsung model at Â£616 (down from Â£749), based on its picture quality and Amazon customer rating.

Its fourth option was for a Sony TV, but it was only 50 inches, leaving us unsure why it had included it.

All the prices stated were correct, but Amazon only searches its own store. Alexa did acknowledge this, encouraging us to check its rivals for better deals, "as prices can vary" and we could get longer guarantees elsewhere.

There are methods that can boost the likelihood of getting a better response, from crafting more informative prompts to upping a chatbot's "thinking" level.

Tech journalist Alex Hughes had better luck than we did when he used ChatGPT's agentic AI mode to find a TV in last year's Black Friday sales.

Agentic AI is still in its early days, but it allows AI to act with more autonomy and carry out multi-step tasks.

The search took about 20 minutes "with very little work on my end needed," Hughes wrote for Tom's Guide, external in November 2025.

A year on, he told the BBC he didn't go through with buying a TV at that time.

He did later purchase one of the seven models ChatGPT had surfaced - but he had found it for a lower price in a bricks-and-mortar shop.

Will he be using AI to find Black Friday deals again this year? "Probably not," he said.

He reckoned it saved him time, but "when you're trying to deal with something like live price data... it will be inconsistent and quite often incorrect".

While we were served up the same brands again and again, in reality there are tonnes of products listed for sale around Black Friday.

Naureen Mohammed, who advises businesses on how to get their products to appear in AI results, told the BBC customers did not see the full picture when they talked to chatbots.

If businesses fail to update their prices when people start hunting for bargains, "last year's prices are served up as today's deals".

"I suspect the whole model for advertising is going to start influencing AI recommendations," she said.

Her own analysis of chatbots' Black Friday tech deals in the UK suggested the big retailers came out on top - with Amazon mentioned in half of responses, and Currys following closely behind.

Her tips include asking an assistant "is that this year's price?" and clicking on the links provided - "because you shouldn't take any advice from the AI system at face value".

But consumer group Which? recently said inaccurate pricing in AI results added more work, external to Black Friday deal-hunting.

"If you ask ChatGPT for a list of 10 laptops under Â£400 and seven of the results are priced higher, you've likely wasted a good chunk of time going through them to discover the bot gave you only three options," said senior researcher Hannah Walsh.

"The opposite is also true - you could miss out on great models in your budget if ChatGPT tells you they are more expensive than they actually are."

Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

Listen: Black Friday - are you really saving money?
