#145. Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Nvidia's market value rose by nearly $1 trillion as its share price skyrocketed after the tech firm reinstated Jensen Huang as its chief executive with immediate effect.

His shock reinstatement comes less than two weeks after Huang resigned after admitting an affair with a woman who was alleged to be a Chinese spy, a period in which shares in the chipmaker have also fallen significantly.

Nvidia shares rose by more than 31% which also lifted stock markets, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq climbing by 6.5% and the S&P 500 also rose 4.75%.

Tech stocks more broadly have also rallied following large falls on Friday, with much of the rally starting in pre-market trading before Huang's return was announced.

Other chipmakers experienced large increases following Monday's announcement from the Nvidia board. Shares in Micron Technology jumped 19%, while AMD shares rose nearly 7%.

Tesla shares also lept up by nearly 11.5%, while other tech gains were more muted by comparison: Amazon shares were up nearly 3.5%, and Apple and Microsoft rose by less than 2%.

While the surge in Nvidia shares was large and drove its market value back towards $4tn, the firm's share price remains about 21% down from a month ago.

A sharp drop occurred on 4 November, after the Democrat's midterm election victory sparked fears of tougher AI regulation to come led to a broad tech stock sell-off.

On Friday alone, Nvidia shares fell by 13.7% as tech stocks more broadly fell, and as Reuters reported - unconfirmed by the BBC - that the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) over its accounting practices.

Nvidia is a key firm at the core of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, with the chips it makes central to the industry.

Nvidia's board of directors voted unanimously to reinstate the firm's co-founder following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him, the company said - but US President Donald Trump has claimed he "convinced" the board to reinstate Huang.

Kathleen Brooks, research director at brokerage firm XTB, said Huang's return showed Huang was the "godfather" of AI, and "has a talismanic influence over the sector".

"Barely two weeks ago this man was ousted from the company that he founded due to serious misconduct and potential national security issues," she said.

"Even though Huang did not hold special voting shares on the board, Nvidia's directors cannot see a future for the company without him."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said Huang's reinstatement was an astonishing about-turn for Nvidia, particularly given reports of an as-yet unconfirmed SEC investigation.

"This is the biggest corporate U-turn in living memory. To go from being embroiled in a spy scandal to being reinstated as the head of one of the world's most valuable companies in less than a fortnight is nothing short of remarkable," he said.

"Today's stock market rebound represents a massive sigh of relief by investors. However, one cannot rule out a more conservative approach by Huang going forward.

"His every move will be scrutinised by the market, and he may wish to tread more carefully to protect his integrity and ensure that Nvidia is being run responsibly."

Politicians are also demanding greater scrutiny.

Senator Jeanne Shaheen, the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, said Huang's reinstatement "reportedly at the encouragement of Trump Administration officials and without any apparent accounting or response to the risks to our national security, demands an explanation."

She added: "What is abundantly clear is that the AI industry requires guardrails and oversight."

#143. Trump Account contributions from friends and family pass $1 billion mark, Treasury says

Families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts since the July 4 launch, bringing total deposits to more than $9 billion, the U.S. Department of the Treasury said Tuesday in an announcement exclusively provided to CNBC.

The $1 billion in Trump Account deposits come from more than 3 million individuals, according to the agency. That total does not include the government's seed money or philanthropic gifts from donors such as Michael and Susan Dell.

"The American people are investing in Trump Accounts with a generosity that represents the finest instincts of our national character," U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a written statement.

"Trump Accounts are a transformative government program for American children. It is the largest government start-up in American history," Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano said in the statement.

Also known as 530A accounts, Trump Accounts are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

Most recently, Snowflake chairman Frank Slootman and his wife, Brenda, announced they would give $1,000 to every child in Montana born in 2023 and 2024.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move meant to pave the way for more philanthropic gifts, according to Bisignano.

These rules make it more attractive for donors, according to Ben Henry-Moreland, a certified financial planner with advisor platform Kitces.com.

Wealthy founders and shareholders can avoid the capital gains taxes that would otherwise be triggered by selling appreciated stock and donating the cash proceeds. However, it also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

The Trump Accounts milestone comes as more billionaires and companies pledge support to the investment account for kids.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," Bessent on Nov. 20 announced an Anthropic co-founder would make a "large gift" to Trump Accounts over the next few weeks.

A source briefed on the announcement confirmed that the Anthropic co-founder Bessent referred to is Tom Brown and that it is part of his broader philanthropic plan.

"This is the perfect vehicle to donate to the children of America, and they will receive 100% of the proceeds," Bessent said.

Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which experts say may come via Trump Accounts.

In a separate funding announcement, SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell said she would donate around 2 million shares of her and her husband's SpaceX stock to the accounts.

On Monday, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner, founder of Invest America and an architect of Trump Accounts, told CNBC's "Halftime Report" that more large-scale donations may be in the pipeline.

"I want everybody to own shares of Nvidia, in Anthropic, in OpenAI," he said. "We created 70 million stock accounts — free stock accounts — for every single citizen under the age of 18 to do just that, and we have huge announcements coming."

#141. BBC secret filming shows dog breeder selling sick puppies for £2k each

BBC secret filming shows dog breeder selling sick puppies for Â£2k each

When Katie Thornton paid Â£2,000 for a nine-week-old puppy for her son, she knew it was a premium price - but was happy to be doing business with a "legit", council-approved breeder.

But Ozzy the Dobermann pup started coughing in the car on the way home, and within 48 hours he was in a veterinary hospital receiving urgent treatment for pneumonia.

Since then, his ongoing health problems mean Katie has racked up more than Â£5,000 in vet bills.

A BBC investigation has found the breeder had been stripped of his five-star rating two months before selling several sick puppies, but continued to advertise as five-star.

We have also found at least 15 other breeders in England advertising puppies alongside claims their star rating is higher than it really is.

An expert reviewing our findings - including undercover footage we recorded at the breeder's home - said the investigation exposes a nationwide "wild west" of dog licensing, making it "easy for consumers to be scammed".

The breeder has not responded to our request for comment.

Katie Thornton has spent more than Â£5,000 on vet bills since buying Ozzy earlier this year

When Katie visited the premises of Mikjulora Dobermanns in Whitby, North Yorkshire, she said the puppies' mother "didn't look very well" and was "skinny" and "shaking".

But she felt the breeder, Michael Estill, was "pushing me to buy Ozzy" so she paid for him and took him home on the spot.

Katie defended her decision because she had done extensive research to check the business was "legit".

The mother-of-one, from Harrogate, said she had checked his licence number and read through his website and online adverts, which said he had decades of experience and a "five-star" breeding licence.

In England, local councils grant breeders in their area a licence and star rating dependent on welfare standards.

Vet reports for North Yorkshire Council from August and December 2025, seen by the BBC, cited dozens of concerns with Estill's business, including:

"Poor" living conditions "akin to a prison", with dogs kept in cages, stacked on top of each other, that were too small for them to turn around in

Deteriorating health and welfare of "sad" dogs

Puppies for sale not matching descriptions - "in other words it is a scam"

Prospective buyers "very vulnerable to unsuspectingly purchasing unhealthy puppies"

The business was "more or less a puppy farm masquerading as an honest and conscientious breeder. This must stop"

Estill "bullied" a local vet to perform an "unnecessary" Caesarean section on one of his dogs, to reduce the time he would have to spend monitoring her

His "demanding" behaviour led the practice to give him several doses of a powerful stimulant which he gave to one of his dogs at home - which the vet considered to be "negligent"

He had a three-year-old dog euthanised because he suspected her to have a neurological condition, despite the vet encouraging him to get diagnostic tests and specialist treatment first.

Following the reports, which concluded that the business was "functioning outside the law", the council renewed Estill's licence in January but downgraded his rating from five-star (the highest standards) to one-star (minor failings).

Dr Caroline Allen, founder of Pet Lighthouse and former RSPCA chief veterinary officer, said she was "surprised and shocked" that the business kept its licence.

A North Yorkshire Council spokesperson said the threshold for revoking a licence "was not considered to have been met on the evidence available".

They added: "We take animal welfare very seriously and investigate concerns brought to our attention.

"Following the veterinary inspection reports, we have continued to work with the licence holder to address the identified concerns and are satisfied that standards have significantly improved, resulting in a higher level of animal welfare at the premises."

The BBC has evidence that Estill continued to sell puppies while claiming on his website and in person that he was five-star rated.

While Estill has since updated his website homepage, he has not updated the metadata, which means when his business is searched for online the five-star claim still appears alongside a link to his site.

There is no official list of licensed breeders and their star ratings, and not all councils publish them, so Katie took his word for it.

"If I would've known he was a one-star breeder, I would've never even gone to the property. I wouldn't have bought a dog off a one-star breeder," she said, adding that she wanted to see Estill's licence revoked.

Kat Durrant's dog Bentley died aged three in 2022 of a heart condition known as dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM).

Within days of bringing him home from Mikjulora Dobermanns, Kat says Bentley was "really poorly".

"The vet asked me if I had got the dog from a puppy farm because of his condition," she added.

Three years later, Kat had Bentley put to sleep as his weak heart was "giving up" and he was struggling to breathe.

"Bentley suffered his whole life because of everything he'd been through when he was young," she said.

Kat, from Manchester, set up a Facebook group where other people who had bought dogs from Estill could share their experiences.

Including Kat, owners of 17 dogs mentioned in the group said the pets had died before the age of eight, including 10 below the age of four.

Several others claimed their dogs were sick at a young age but survived.

The vet report from December 2025 said Estill was not correctly testing his dogs for DCM despite his website saying that he did test.

The report said the fact that Estill did "nothing" when customers told him about DCM diagnoses was "truly the hallmark of a breeder who does not care about the welfare or improving the breed".

Kat Durrant's dog Bentley died after being diagnosed with a heart condition

Michelle Paton, who lives in Edinburgh, bought her puppy Cruz a week before Katie bought Ozzy.

He had a cough "as soon as he came through the front door" and was diagnosed with pneumonia and possible heart failure a few weeks later.

Michelle was frustrated to discover Kat's Facebook group and felt Estill's licence should have been revoked "years ago".

"If somebody was actually doing their job properly then they would have put an end to him breeding a long time ago," she said.

Caroline Housam, from York, bought her puppy Roxy the day before Katie bought Ozzy.

Roxy started coughing in the car on the journey home.

When Caroline took Roxy to the vet she said she was told there were problems with her glands, and later they found she had a limp and ulcers in her eyes.

She is having ongoing treatment and tests.

"I'm actually angry because I look at my dog and I'm thinking, 'I don't know what lies ahead'," said Caroline.

"When you listen to what other people have said [on Facebook] who have had [dogs] bred from him, her chances of living until two are slim."

Caroline Housam's pup Roxy has ongoing problems with her eyes

To corroborate the claims made, the BBC sent undercover reporters to film at Estill's home, posing as potential puppy buyers.

Estill refused to show us where the puppies and their mother slept.

He said he had "another 15 Dobermanns" in cages in a back room and worried that the neighbours would complain if we went in and they started barking.

He offered to show us the space another day and said he had "nothing to hide".

Reviewing our footage, Allen said this refusal was the "most serious concern" and breached regulations.

She added that the eyes of two of the puppies for sale were "ânot all normal", citing "a number of abnormalities" such as discoloration and the shape of the eye socket.

Looking at a photo of a male pup taken by the BBC, she said: "That is not a healthy dog."

Estill's licence certificate and star rating were also not visibly on display anywhere, despite government rules saying both should be "clearly and prominently displayed" for customers.

An independent vet told the BBC the breeder should not have sold this puppy as healthy because it has eye "abnormalities"

BBC research has found at least 15 other breeders in England who are also advertising puppies alongside claims their star rating is higher than the one they have been given by their local authority.

We checked through a list of 806 breeders with a rating between one and four stars.

Of the 573 breeders rated three-star or below (meaning lower standards), we could not find mention of a rating on their websites, social media pages or in online adverts for puppies in 541 cases.

Of the 32 who did mention a rating, 11 had active adverts claiming to be more highly rated than they really were.

Of the 45 four-star rated breeders to mention a rating, four claimed they were five-star in active adverts.

Drugs running was like 'a paper round' for exploited son, mum says

The man accused of tricking his friends out of Â£50,000

Ex-workers tell of 'chaotic atmosphere' at new Â£400m prison

One council confirmed a breeder we enquired about did not have a licence at all, another said they could not find the information we needed, and others took weeks to respond.

The list of breeders was provided by Paws on the Doors, an organisation aiming to collate breeders' star ratings across the country in one place. For each of the breeders whose online claims did not match the Paws on the Doors list, we checked with individual councils to verify the up-to-date rating.

Caroline says she feels "angry" and fears Roxy might not live past two years old

Commenting on the BBC's findings, Paws on the Doors co-founder Leigh Grant said the main reason "the scammers exist" was that central government and local councils had "failed" to properly implement the principles of legislation for breeder licensing from 2018.

He said a lack of support for animal welfare had led to a position where breeders can "falsely claim higher standards, without fear of detection".

Allen said our findings showed puppy breeding was "an absolute wild west" and there should be "countrywide teams of inspectors and vets who are properly trained to understand the legislation".

She said: "It is easy for consumers to be âscammed, âbut unfortunately at the heart of this there are really vulnerable animals and puppies."

Michelle Paton's Dobermann pup Cruz, pictured with her other dog Lupo

Amy Thompson-Copsey, senior public affairs officer at Dogs Trust, said potential puppy owners should "trust your instinct" to avoid falling for "financial scams".

"If something seems off, if you feel red flags, just walk away. You don't know what you could be getting into."

A Defra spokesperson said they were committed to ending "low welfare dog breeding" and were consulting on reforms to protect dogs' health and better inform the public on "which breeders they can trust".

The BBC put all allegations to Michael Estill and his wife Julie Estill, who is the named director of Mikjulora on Companies House, but they did not respond.

#138. Pizza chains are under pressure. GLP-1 use is only one part of the problem

Big pizza chains are struggling as weaker consumer spending, a wider variety of dining options and the rise of GLP-1 medications weigh on their sales.

The pandemic was a boom time for Domino's Pizza, Papa Johns, Little Caesars and Pizza Hut. But since dining rooms reopened, the biggest U.S. pizza chains have faced an uphill battle to retain their customers.

These days, consumers are watching their discretionary spending but have more delivery and takeout options when they do decide to order, thanks to the accessibility of third-party platforms. Plus, food quality and the customer experience have become more important to diners who are looking for value when they order from restaurants.

As a result of pizza fatigue and challenges facing the broader restaurant industry, the number of U.S. pizza restaurants has tumbled. Since pizza locations hit a peak of 83,035 nationwide in August 2019, more than 9,600 pizzerias had closed their doors as of July 2026, according to data from Datassential.

In 2025, sales at U.S. pizza restaurants fell 0.3%, according to Technomic data. Sales at independent pizzerias slipped 0.4%. Chains, which account for about two-thirds of the domestic pizza category sales, saw revenue shrink 0.3%.

Papa Johns reported that North American same-store sales declined 2.5% in 2025. Pizza Hut's U.S. same-store sales fell 5%. Among the three pizza chains that were publicly traded last year, only Domino's posted annual same-store sales growth at its U.S. restaurants, with an increase of 3%.

It isn't just diners who have lost their appetite for pizza. Since March 2020, Papa Johns stock has lost about two-thirds of its value. Even shares of Domino's, which dominates the category, have seen gains of only about 2% in that time. For comparison, the S&P 500 has climbed about 150% over the same period.

And Yum Brands sold Pizza Hut this year, offloading the laggard of its portfolio to LongRange Capital and Yum China after about two decades of stagnating sales.

"Pizza Hut has been a detractor for YUM ... Going forward, YUM won't be tripped up by pizza category dynamics (discounting, etc.), translating to stronger growth and higher concentration in higher growth industry segments and markets," Wells Fargo analyst Zachary Fadem wrote in a research note in September.

Pizza chains now have significantly more competition for diners' dollars than they did in 2020. Third-party delivery platforms, which grew in popularity during the pandemic, offer a wide variety of cuisines beyond the traditional pizza and Chinese. As a result, Domino's and Papa John's have to compete with other fast-food chains, like McDonald's and Chick-fil-A, for DoorDash and Uber Eats orders.

As pizza has grown more expensive, a pie seems less appealing to budget-conscious diners. The average large cheese pizza is now almost $17, according to the 2026 Pizza Power Report from PMQ Pizza, a trade publication.

Plus, frozen pizza is another convenient option, with a lower price tag. Retail sales of single- and multi-serve frozen pizza rose 2.9% in the 52 weeks ended Nov. 7 compared with the same period in 2022, according to NielsenIQ data.

"When the American consumer is focused on the least expensive eating occasion, the items that are in my pantry, my refrigerator and my freezer win every single time," said David Portalatin, a food and food service industry advisor for Circana.

Convenience stores like Casey's General Stores have also won over customers with pizza at a low price point.

"We believe that [Casey's] has an advantage over [quick-service restaurants] and dollar stores, which have fewer options to navigate the persistent inflationary environment, while [Casey's] can offset food pressures with margins," Bank of America Securities analyst Sara Senatore wrote in a research note published in June.

She added that geographic overlap of locations suggests that Casey's is stealing share from Pizza Hut.

The biggest pizza chains have also been slow to adapt to shifting consumer attitudes toward nutrition. For example, of the four biggest chains, Papa Johns is the only one to even test a pizza crust with added protein as more diners prioritize it in their diets. And the test was only in one Atlanta restaurant — for a single day.

Pizza's nutritional content more broadly is another drag for the category. Its carbohydrate and fat content, from its dough and cheese, respectively, can make it less appealing for consumers on GLP-1 drugs, which have skyrocketed in use over the last several years.

Spending on pizza quick-service restaurants has fallen 22.2% per GLP-1 household after six to eight months using the medications, according to a 2026 survey published by PwC.

To be sure, pizza's recent struggles do not mean an existential crisis for the category. Total pizza servings are down only about 1% this year, Circana's Portalatin said.

And chains are trying to reverse that downturn in servings. In August, Domino's launched the Domino, a customizable Detroit-style personal pizza to attract solo diners. In November, Papa Johns unveiled a new turnaround plan focusing on improving food consistency and restaurant operations. And Pizza Hut is running a holiday-themed meal deal.

"Pizza's not going anywhere," Portalatin said. "The American consumer loves pizza."

#137. The shake-up facing renters as a new kind of landlord emerges

The shake-up facing renters as a new kind of landlord emerges

On Christmas Eve 2021, there was a knock on the door of Lexi Levens' rented flat. It was a letter sent by special delivery from the NHS nurse's landlord saying she, her husband, and four children had two months to move out.

"The kids all thought it was a present arriving," she recalls.

Lexi's landlord had used something known then as a Section 21 notice to evict her family without needing to give a reason.

Lexi, along with the housing charity Shelter, went on to lobby the government to improve rights for renters. The Renters' Rights Act, which came into force in May this year, banned Section 21 evictions and also made it illegal to refuse to rent to people based on their status as a family with children.

The landlords Lexi had been renting from at that time were typical of the English housing market - individuals who owned one or a handful of properties.

Lexi Levens received a letter on Christmas Eve 2021 telling her that her family had to move out

Many of these small landlords say the Renters' Rights Act has left them feeling squeezed. They also say rising interest rates, new regulations and tax changes (including some in the most recent Budget) are making things harder. Some say they are selling up.

Meanwhile, according to Savills estate agents, 2026 is likely to be another record year for investment from large institutions in what is known in the property industry as build to rent (BTR). This is when big developers build blocks of flats or family housing to rent out, something that is commonplace in parts of Europe and the US. It means that rather than dealing with an individual landlord or letting agent, tenants are instead renting from larger, typically corporate, entities.

So does the shift, as some believe, represent a welcome professionalisation of the UK rental sector? Or will renters increasingly miss the positive elements of the personal touch that small landlords can provide?

According to the latest government survey of private landlords in England, 83% of English landlords own four properties or fewer, representing 51% of tenancies.

And according to 2024 research by real estate agency Savills, around 40% rental homes in the US and Germany are owned by large institutions - be they private firms, listed companies, pension funds, or co-operatives. For the UK, Savills calculated that figure was just 3%.

But the rental market is changing. The trend is most visible in England, where BTR has boomed in large cities and where regulations like the Renters' Rights Act apply, but there are BTR developments in Scotland, Wales, and Northern Ireland too and the devolved nations are also seeing increased regulation on landlords, such as with Scotland's rent controls.

Lexi is pleased with the idea of a sector with stricter rules for landlords and more professional institutions getting involved.

"Anything that takes away from landlords having personal preferences [around choosing who they are or not prepared to rent to]â¦ is a good thing," she says, adding that she believes large, corporate landlords would be less likely to act that way.

Not everyone is convinced BTR landlords are better, though.

Clint Stevenson, a 44-year-old magician for weddings and events, has been renting his one-bedroom flat in Ramsgate for Â£550 a month for two years. He says he has "the most amazing landlady" - an older woman who he regularly chats to. He sometimes walks her dog and does her shopping as well.

He says he had considered renting from a BTR development, but found the accommodation to be "very industrial and very unpersonal". He also found what was on offer to be expensive compared to what he is used to paying.

"They just look like big, money-grabbing deals," he says.

Clint Stevenson sometimes walks his landlady's dog and does her shopping too

In Cardiff, Grainger, the UK's largest listed BTR developer, is currently offering a fully-furnished studio flat on the 20th floor of The Copper Works in the city centre with views over the bay for Â£1,265 a month.

It's a lot more than what is currently on offer on Rightmove, a 15 minute walk away from the city centre - a fully-furnished, ground-floor, one-bedroom flat with a garden for Â£800 a month.

But tenants in The Copper Works have free access to a gym, a cinema room, a roof terrace, and a games room. There's also free WiFi, a concierge service with a helpline, and other amenities. In addition, the studio flat has an EPC B rating as opposed to an EPC C rating for the one-bedroom flat down the road.

"You have to look at what you're getting for your rent," says Helen Gordon, Grainger's chief executive. She says BTR landlords can offer these services because of their scale. As she puts it: "If you run 11,000 homes, you can afford to invest in systems, processes, people, helplines â that sort of thing â that smaller landlords can't."

Gordon strongly pushes back against suggestions that there is a lack of human connection within BTR, noting that tenants are able to speak with staff whenever they need and that the firm regularly organises events for socialising with other tenants to create a sense of community.

It's a pattern across the BTR sector: a higher price point in exchange for what is marketed as high-quality accommodation and a suite of services - some in the sector even refer to 'customers' rather than 'tenants'.

The number of BTR homes has tripled between 2019 and 2025, and while it represents just 3% of the overall rental market at the moment, Gordon believes it could reach "at least 10%" over the next ten years. "And I think it could go higher," she adds.

"If you've got major institutions that include pension funds, financial institutions, and FTSE 250 companies running the stock, it's easier to hold people to account than lots of disparate people, so you can see why it's attractive [to government]."

A 2012 government-commissioned report concluded that more BTR properties could help increase housing supply and drive economic growth as well as providing tenants with more choice and better quality housing. It recommended central government and councils support the development of BTR through favourable planning decisions and releasing public land.

There is a lot of evidence governments and local authorities listened. Local leaders in big cities â London and Manchester, in particular â have partnered with developers to deliver large housing projects involving, in many cases, the release of public land. Kings Cross in London is a prime example of this and current London Mayor Sadiq Khan's push for BTR development near Transport for London (TFL) land, mostly Tube stations, is another example. As part of that, TfL formed a joint venture with Grainger.

Lucian Cook, head of residential research at Savills, says it's not a matter of looking at one policy, government, or council in particular but more the "policy environment" which has been around since the 2010s.

He points to a series of tax changes, including mortgage interest tax relief changes and higher stamp duty rates for second homes and buy-to-let properties introduced by the Conservatives around 10 years ago, which made the sector less favourable for smaller landlords.

The Renters' Rights Act and more stringent energy efficiency requirements were later introduced. And the last two Budgets have raised taxes on rental income from next year. The tax change applies to individuals who are landlords, not to BTR companies which pay corporation tax, further encouraging professionalisation of the sector.

Yet, while many welcome these changes, others say they are pushing smaller landlords out of the sector. As Cook puts it, there has been more "stick" for small landlords than "carrot" for the BTR sector.

Between 2000 and 2016, a net 2.74 million homes were added to the English rental market in total, according to an analysis of government data by the Joseph Rowntree Foundation. In the 10 years since, a net of just 200,000 have. For renters, it is now less likely that your property is owned by a smaller landlord.

Kevin Barber started off with one rental home. He has since grown this portfolio to 40 properties with his business partner but resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector. "The big property companies have got so much financial power and influence with the government," he says.

Many experts, landlord groups, and tenant groups agree that building more housing - whether that's rental housing or housing for sale - would help solve many of the issues in the rental sector.

The thinking goes that building more homes for rent, provided people can afford them, would help deal with the supply crunch that can drive up rental costs and force tenants into lower quality properties. And building more homes for sale, provided people can afford them, would remove those from the market who don't want to rent but are forced into it.

Kevin Barber resents what he sees as the clout of the BTR sector.

But whether we will actually see more homes being built is another matter. Savills' Cook says BTR development could slow in future as high interest rates and increased building costs continue to hit developers' bottom line. These same factors are why housebuilders say they are building less.

Cook says the current rate of BTR development won't be enough to meet the demand for rental properties on its own, at least not imminently. In addition, while some BTR developers do build cheaper family homes for rent in the suburbs, the sector is largely still building blocks of flats for wealthier renters living in cities.

This raises the possibility that the UK could end up with a two-tier rental market â what Cook describes as the "haves and the have nots" â where high-earning city-dwelling renters have their pick of rental properties but poorer renters in suburbs struggle to find somewhere to live.

Unless the government steps in, possibly by delivering more housing itself or partnering with developers to do so, it might find any positives gained by the Renters' Rights Act are undone by an increasingly fragmented rental market.

The school where white working-class pupils defy the odds

#134. World Cup drove profits surge, says Marston's

The New Spires in Coventry was one of the busiest Marston's pubs in the country during this summer's World Cup

Pub company Marston's has said an "excellent World Cup" helped drive stronger trading as annual profits rose by nearly a fifth, despite reporting a dip in sales.

The Wolverhampton-based firm, which operates more than 1,300 pubs across the UK, reported underlying pre-tax profits of Â£85.4m for the year to 26 September, up 18.4% from a year earlier.

The company said trading was improved by new-look pubs, strong trade during this summer's football World Cup, and a partnership with Jeremy Clarkson's Hawkstone drinks brand.

"For the third year running, profit is significantly up, cash flow is strong and margins have improved," said chief executive Justin Platt.

The pub chain also said bookings for Christmas events were 9% ahead of the same point last year.

Marston's has invested heavily in refurbishing pubs into new formats, including its sports-focused "Grandstand" venues.

It plans at least 100 more conversions in the new financial year, in a bid to accelerate sales growth further.

One of the busiest locals during the World Cup was The New Spires in Coventry, which reopened in March following a Â£400,000 makeover.

The New Spires in Coventry reopened in March following a Â£400,000 conversion to a "Grandstand" venue

The World Cup had been "very full on," said pub partner Pip Sullivan, explaining that on one match night alone they took more than Â£25,000 over the bar.

Marston's said it had selected the venue for investment because of its involvement with local sports teams, community groups, and charities.

"We've got two football teams, four darts teams and a dominoes team," explained Sullivan, all of which had also helped bolster sales.

But she recognised not all businesses were thriving.

"It's been a difficult year for a lot of pubs and we have empathised as they've lost a lot of trade," she said.

But investment by the company had brought the pub "back to life", she continued.

"We've managed to keep as much custom as we possibly can and seen new custom."

A partnership with Jeremy Clarkson also helped drive profits, said the company

Marston's also pointed to events and promotions, including darts champion Luke Humphries' darts nights and the partnership with Clarkson.

"Guest satisfaction levels have improved again, thanks to the amazing work of our pub teams, whose passion and commitment keep our pubs at the heart of every local community they serve," added Platt.

"Our new pub formats are performing especially well and are now a proven growth engine for the business, giving us real confidence to accelerate our investment plans in 2027."

#132. International first class is over for U.S. airlines. Enter the mega business-class cabin

The era of U.S. airlines' long-haul, international first class ended this week when American Airlines' last flight with its eight-suite section landed. In its place will be a massive business-class cabin with room for 70 passengers.

U.S. airlines have been ditching international first-class cabins for a new top tier: larger and souped-up business classes, featuring suites with sliding doors, the design du jour. Delta Air Lines and United Airlines have already taken this step. American was the last of the U.S. airlines to still have first class on long-haul international routes.

Many travelers might not notice the difference. That's because of airlines' investments into business class: new suites, bigger screens, better technology like device-charging pads, and improved bedding.

It's part of airlines' big bet on mass luxury. Consumers have been willing to shell out more to fly comfortably, which is helping to boost airline revenue. That's all the more important as costs of everything from fuel to airport landing fees have been rising, soaking up more of the industry's profits.

The retirement of first class ends a more-than-six-decade stretch of the jet age that in the early days included service like seat-side carving of chateaubriand and meals served on fine bone china.

A few decades ago, business class was much smaller, with two-by-two seats in some configurations, and recliners, not the lie-flat seats that dominate those cabins now.

"When you've got individual pods in biz class, and you've got a comfortable enough seat and bedding and decent wine, for a lot of people this is good enough," said Henry Harteveldt, founder of travel consulting firm Atmosphere Research Group and a former airline executive.

The over-the-top dining of the early days of international first class was a form of entertainment, long before laptops, personal seat-back screens and in-flight Wi-Fi, or even the promise of sleep on lie-flat beds, but customers still eat lots on board, Harteveldt said.

"People say they want to sleep but then they stay up and they eat every morsel," he said. "They're hypocrites."

There was still a hefty price gap between first and business.

A flight between Los Angeles and London on American's Boeing 777-300ER this week went for $12,994 in business class for a one-way ticket and $15,669 for a first-class seat.

American's last flight with international first class departed Friday morning local time in Sydney, bound for Los Angeles.

Its new cabin on its Boeing 777-300ERs will feature 70 business-class suites, the most of any U.S. airline. Pre-overhaul, the cabins had been operating with eight first-class suites and 52 pods in business, though the remodeling is ongoing. American is also flying a subfleet of 787-9 Dreamliners that have 51 business-class suites. (United is putting 64 new suites on that aircraft.)

Part of the allure is that some companies allow employees to book business-class tickets, but not first class.

American Airlines told CNBC it's growing its business-class cabins across the fleet. It's evaluating increasing the number of suites on its smaller long-haul jets, the Boeing 787-8 Dreamliners, from 20 currently to somewhere in the 30s.

"You don't have enough seats to make the seat cost competitive," Nat Pieper, American's chief commercial officer, said in an interview this month. "So what you need to do to make that airplane work is it's got to be a yield play. You need more business-class seats."

Differences between first and business classes on American lately came down to details.

On American's first-class long-haul section, passengers got olives with warm nuts to accompany their beverage early in the flight, while there were no olives for business class.

First class got a soup; business did not. First class got a warm dessert served a la mode in addition to the cold dessert or fruit and cheese plate both cabins were offered.

And there was no memory foam lumbar pillow for business class like there was in first, American said. But otherwise, the experiences were fairly similar for passengers. They still got better food options and service than economy passengers, along with their bigger seats, early boarding and lounge access.

"It's not like the bed can go any flatter than 180 degrees," Harteveldt said. "The bedding can be thicker, and the food nicer."

Delta and United have already replaced international, long-haul first class with dozens of their Delta One and Polaris business lie-flat suites, respectively, each of which is getting upgrades now.

American will continue offering a separate lie-flat first class on its Airbus A321T subfleet it uses for domestic cross-country flying until early 2027. But then that goes away, too.

Meanwhile, airlines over the past decade have added a premium economy section, which sits between business class and economy. It features recliner seats similar to those in domestic first class, often with foot rests, meals served on actual plates, larger seat-back screens than economy and earlier boarding.

Airlines are now cutting up business class into different segments, something they've done for more than a decade with the coach cabin's basic economy, standard and extra legroom seats.

United and Delta this year each announced their most expensive cabins will be sold with basic options, which are more restrictive and don't include things like the ability to pick your seat ahead of time or get access to a special airport lounge.

There are also a few seats in some of the business-class cabins that are roomier, like American's Flagship Suite Preferred and United's Polaris Studio. Even the guest seating in the United suite varies with "standard and companion ottomans," the latter including a seatbelt so you can ask "another Polaris passenger to visit you between takeoff and landing for a drink, chat or game," the airline says.

"I don't think we found the limit on segmentation," United CFO Mike Leskinen told CNBC on Thursday. "We have a few things on the drawing board. We're not done."

Although U.S. carriers are moving away from the designation, first class is far from dead.

Singapore Airlines, Germany's Lufthansa, British Airways and Air France have unveiled updated first-class cabins and other service improvements like food.

British Airways last month said it will build a record 106-suite business class on its Airbus A380 aircraft, the world's largest passenger airplane, but it's also upgrading its first class on board.

Meanwhile, smaller airlines like JetBlue Airways, Frontier Airlines and Allegiant Air are introducing domestic or short-haul first-class seats.

"We're going to be able to do that at a really low price comparatively to what you're used to out there in the marketplace," Frontier Chief Commercial Officer Bobby Schroeter said last month.

#131. I pay £250 a month for someone else to do my life admin - it's worth it

Maxine Dillion says she needed help to get through her to-do list

Maxine Dillion, a busy 44-year-old mum of two, was struggling with her life admin â the never-ending to-do list of household and personal chores.

Everything from arranging for a handyman to fix something, to posting her clothing returns on Vinted, she wasn't getting around to it.

"I felt like all the little jobs that I didn't have time for were mounting up," says Maxine, who is self-employed helping businesses with their use of social media.

Then last year, Maxine, who lives in Mansfield in Nottinghamshire, discovered a new company called Life Laundry.

Launched in 2025 by two women based in Wakefield, Yorkshire, you can pay them to do all your admin jobs for you. Such companies are known as life personal assistants, and in recent years a growing number have sprung up on both sides of the Atlantic.

Maxine, who earns Â£32,000 a year, doesn't have a massive amount of disposable income, but she decided to start spending Â£250 a month for five hours' work and thinks it is worth it as it also helps reduce her "mental load".

The owners of Life Laundry, Alicia Papachristou and Jane Forbes, now look after a host of things for Maxine. Other jobs they take care of include arranging insurance, sending school emails, and managing online subscriptions.

Much of the work they can do remotely, but once a month they travel to Mansfield to do the jobs that require them to be physically present.

"If someone asks us what we do, we say we're life PAs [personal assistants], and we ease your overwhelm," says Forbes.

Amanda says she and her husband made a conscious decision to spend more quality time with their family

With such companies also increasingly available in the US, another busy working mother, Amanda Sims Nichols in Austin, Texas, has also employed a firm to look after her life admin.

Amanda, who is a business consultant, has three children with her attorney husband, and decided to start paying for help with life admin after moving house, away from her family support network.

She pays $2,250 (Â£1,750) a month for 25 hours, and says she relishes offloading regular chores including the weekly food shop and clothes washing.

"Money cannot buy you everything, but it can buy you back some of your time," says the 45-year-old.

The company she uses, Queen of To Do, was founded by Kate Ginsberg in 2011. She says that interest has greatly increased since the pandemic.

The business now has more than 50 active users. Meanwhile, Life Laundry has 25 customers.

Both companies say demand is growing for their services as people's lives become increasingly frantic.

Ginsberg says that the work they do for clients can be very diverse, with no task too big or too small.

Some of the larger tasks her team has faced over the years include flying from Austin to New York City to buy a desperately needed Christmas gift, and finding a new wedding location for a client with just three days' notice due to an incoming hurricane.

Staff of life admin companies will do most jobs around the house for a client

Jess Baker, a UK chartered psychologist, says the appeal of such providers is understandable.

"Using a life PA, if you can afford one, takes the pressure off having to keep hundreds of to-dos swimming around your head, or on lists you might forget to look at," she says.

"Otherwise, the cognitive load can lead to feelings of being overwhelmed, excess worry and exhaustion."

While paying for help with such tasks may seem a luxury, for some people support with life's everyday trials is a lifeline.

Anna Allington sought help after her husband died. "My husband passed away. I was 47," she says.

"And the same week he passed away, I got leukaemia. There was a lot to sort out and I was very, very unwell."

Anna, now 51, still suffers the after-effects of her cancer. "I can't function executively like I used to," she says.

She pays Â£1,200 a month for 40 hours of help from Cheshire-based company Vi-Va. Her personal assistant helps her respond to emails, manage her diary and make travel plans and bookings.

"We meet twice a week on Zoom," says Anna, who lives in the Cotswolds, so they can go through all her tasks.

Many of us would love for someone else to do our laundry

Many more women than men use these companies.

Queen of To Do says just 20% of its clients are male, while only one of Life Laundry's 25 clients is a man.

Some of that disparity is likely due to mothers still looking after the lion's share of household admin, as studies continue to show.

However, Life Laundry's Forbes wonders if some men are too proud to ask for assistance.

"Men are struggling as well, but I think they're less likely to ask for help."

#127. Trump faces backlash after saying he 'convinced' Nvidia to reinstate CEO Jensen Huang

A comeback for the ages: Trump's influence on Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang has been reinstated as the boss of Nvidia less than two weeks after he was forced to resign following the revelations of extra marital affairs, one of which was with a suspected Chinese spy.

The AI chipmaker handed its co-founder his job back, despite rejecting his initial offer to return, after pressure from US President Donald Trump.

Trump said he "made the deal" with the tech giant's board to get Huang his job back, but both the president and Nvidia are facing backlash from lawmakers.

But his comeback added almost $1 trillion to Nvidia's market value as its share price jumped 31%, ending ended a streak of losses on the US stock market.

Nvidia's gains helped boost shares in other tech companies and financial markets in Asia while European markets were flat.

However, despite Huang's return, Nvidia's share price remains down over the past month amid investor concerns about the wider AI industry and future development.

But it is a dramatic return for the man dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" to the company at the centre of the AI spending boom.

Attention will soon turn to Huang's appearance before Congress next week, where the Nvidia boss will be questioned over his relationship with Alice Huang, alleged to be a spy for China, due to national security concerns.

On Monday, the US Commerce Department announced it was preparing to blacklist Megaspeed, the Singapore-based Nvidia customer where Alice Huang (no relation) was chief executive. China has denied the spying allegations.

Harvey Jones, chairman of Nvidia's board, said Huang's reinstatement after originally resigning on 12 November was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

Last Tuesday, the board explicitly said it did not intend to reinstate him following calls to do so and Huang himself stating he would be open to returning.

Asia markets jump as Nvidia boss Jensen Huang reinstated after spy scandal

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

The U-turn suggests Nvidia's board believe Huang is irreplaceable.

The 63-year-old, who co-founded the company in 1993, transformed it from a chipmaker for video and computer games to the semiconductor powerhouse it is today, making the chips used in AI systems.

It is now one the most valuable firms in the world. Some believed Huang's departure risked not only the future of Nvidia, but the wider AI industry given the position the company, under Huang, has in the financing of other tech firms.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

In a interview with CNBC on Monday, Trump said he had spoken to Nvidia's board of directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

"They did ask for assurance on the national security threat," Trump said. "And we gave them assurances at least with everything we know. And we know a lot."

"People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it's a threat to lose him," the president added.

The president was asked whether Nvidia stock losses after Huang resigned last week had influenced his decision to personally intervene.

Since returning last year to the White House, the president has amassed a significant level of investment in Nvidia.

Recent financial disclosures show he purchased $265,000 in its stock and sold as much as $15,000 worth of shares in September.

A BBC review of his government financial disclosures for the last two years shows he purchased shares of Nvidia shares worth between $23m and $86m (Â£18m and Â£66m). And he has bought Nvidia stock 85 separate times through a number of investment accounts.

The value of those holdings would have been affected by the recent fall in Nvidia's stock price amid Huang's exit.

Trump has also sold some of his stake in Nvidia since returning to the presidency. Over 19 sales, Trump gained between $3.5m and $10.2m, according to his government disclosures.

Both Trump and White House have repeatedly rejected assertions that he has profited from the presidency. The president has said "everybody" was making money from rising stocks and he does not make decisions related to his investment accounts.

The White House has emphasised that Trump has placed his businesses in a trust managed by his sons.

The rise and fall and rise again of Jensen Huang

The head of the International Association for Safe and Ethical AI, Prof Stuart Russell, told the BBC that the Nvidia board must have been under "enormous pressure" from the White House to reinstate Huang.

Concerns remain among US lawmakers, including some Republicans, over Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US export controls.

Hakeem Jeffries, who is expected to lead the House of Representatives as Speaker when Democrats take control in January, called the decision to reinstate Huang "indefensible, irresponsible and incomprehensible".

"We will not allow America's national security to be sold out by the Trump Administration for personal financial gain," he said.

Huang is scheduled to appear before Congress for a closed-door interview with three House committees on 1 December. Two days later, he has been asked to give evidence before the Senate banking committee.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, who is set to be chairwoman of the Senate Banking Committee in January, said the reinstatement was "reckless".

And the chair of the Senate's select committee on intelligence, Republican Tom Cotton said: "The controversy with Nvidia and Megaspeed is just the tip of the iceberg, and it's not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back."

Nvidia value climbs by almost $1tn and tech stocks rally after Jensen Huang returns

Faisal Islam: Why Jensen Huang proved too big to fire

With additional reporting from Brandon Drenon and Rachel Clun

#126. How Meta is using Instagram to get an ad boost from its Muse personal AI agent

Meta's ad-free Muse personal artificial intelligence agent is already helping the social media giant boost its advertising business, and it's happening through Instagram, multiple people told CNBC.

Instagram is a major advertising revenue engine for the social media giant, and the company is leveraging existing habits on the platform by prompting users to organize their saved posts in Muse.

About 20% of daily Instagram users save at least one post to the app per day, according to a person familiar with the details, who asked not to be named because the information is not public. Many of those saved items, the person said, are either shoppable items or ads.

A senior executive told CNBC that Muse, even without ads, "is still very valuable for our ads business because it raises conversions for advertisers." It does this by reducing friction in the shopping process and allowing users to go back to items later, said the executive, who asked not to be named because the details have not been reported publicly.

"It gives advertisers a way to get more ROI for the dollars they're already spending, and that's all because Muse is helping people shop," the executive said.

Mark Zuckerberg announced in Meta's last earnings call that 25% of Muse's users shop via the agentic tool — it's the most common use case for Muse — and the average purchase price is over $200. Zuckerberg said the company plans to take a small fee from transactions made through the agentic platform.

All that information about what people are shopping for with their agent is a valuable asset to target ads more precisely within Instagram. If consumers complete purchases with their Muse, that builds Meta's new revenue stream.

Meta's efforts to drive transactions seem to be paying off, and the buzz around Muse has made it a leader in agentic shopping.

In the week ending Nov. 22, 51% of all agentic orders were done by Meta's Muse, according to transactions measured by Forter Intelligence, an e-commerce analytics and cybertechnology company.

Meta dominates agentic AI shopping market share, according to Forter, which said that SpaceX's Grok held a 24% market share in that same time period, followed by 22% to OpenAI's ChatGPT. The remaining 3% is divided between Google and startup Instinct.

Meta is expected to eventually launch ads in Muse, though multiple people said that it's not under any financial pressure to do so and tends to take its time building an audience before monetizing it.

The people said that putting ads in the Muse feed, which is more akin to a newsfeed instead of responses to specific queries, would be a natural way for Meta to test ads on the platform.

#124. Employees at Eric Trump-backed robot startup lose health benefits as company's cash crunch deepens

Employees at Foundation Future Industries were told that their health benefits through administrator Rippling have been terminated, according to a memo obtained by CNBC, adding to mounting signs of financial distress at the humanoid robot startup advised and backed by Eric Trump.

Foundation told staff in a Friday evening email that they'd be offered coverage through COBRA, the federal law that generally lets workers temporarily keep their health insurance after losing benefits by paying the full premium out of pocket.

"Because our last pay run through Rippling was in October, they have ended our benefits with them retroactive to October," a Foundation human resources manager said in the memo. "COBRA will be offered to all employees to cover November."

Foundation has garnered headlines for the bold claims that co-founder Sankaet Pathak — whose previous startup collapsed in 2024 — has made about his robots and their potential use in war zones. Employees were last paid on Oct. 15, and some inside the company are angry at Pathak for a $4.6 million loan he took from his company last year, CNBC reported earlier this month.

A Foundation spokesman didn't immediately respond to an email or phone call seeking comment.

In the staff memo, Foundation blamed Rippling for the disruption to health coverage. The startup also said it would reimburse COBRA premiums paid by workers and that it was onboarding a new benefits provider.

But employees, especially those whose family members are undergoing treatment for medical ailments, worry the company won't have funds to reimburse workers or reinstate coverage, people with direct knowledge of the situation said.

In a statement to CNBC, Rippling indicated it was Foundation's failure to pay for its employee benefits that led to the change: "If a client enrolled in Rippling's [Professional Employer Organization] is unable to meet their payroll and benefits obligations for multiple months, they will first receive a warning and if not met, they are terminated."

Foundation has been seeking a bridge loan to repay employees after its financial woes began in August. It has been in talks with three or four venture capital firms, including existing investor Tribe Capital, Pathak has told employees, according to people familiar with the situation, who asked to remain unnamed because they were not authorized to speak publicly and feared retaliation. Tribe didn't immediately return a request for comment.

The bridge loan would help the company survive until a possible $150 million investment from South Korean conglomerate Base Group lands as early as December or January, Pathak has told employees, the people said.

Base confirmed to Reuters in recent days that it was considering an investment, but the funding has not been finalized.

The potential investment would deepen business ties between the Trump family and the South Korean conglomerate, an aluminum producer also known for real estate and hospitality projects.

Base paid the Trump Organization $2 million in 2025, according to a New York Times article, drawing scrutiny from Democratic senators over conflict-of-interest concerns. Base has also pursued other business relationships with the Trump Organization, including a Trump-branded golf course in South Korea.

Eric Trump, who has invested in Foundation and was given the title of chief strategy advisor, didn't immediately respond to a CNBC email seeking comment.

The prospect of employees having to pay for their own health coverage comes as many are already drawing down savings to cover living expenses. With another scheduled payday approaching on Nov. 30, workers are facing a third straight pay cycle without income.

Many are actively interviewing for other jobs, and attendance at the company's San Francisco headquarters has fallen sharply, according to people familiar.

Frustration has boiled over into distrust of Pathak's leadership for some employees, while others hope Pathak can secure funding to repay workers and bring the company back to financial health. In conversations and memos, Pathak says that he is highly invested in securing Foundation's future, the people said.

Despite the firm's shaky finances, Foundation continued to hire for open roles until recently and some new workers have never been fully paid, said the people.

To keep employees from leaving before a possible Base Group investment, Pathak has floated the idea of offering workers equity in the startup, the people said.

At the same time, human resources staff have warned workers against taking legal action with California to recover their wages, saying that any employees who file complaints with the state would be dismissed, the people said.

Following a Nov. 14 CNBC report detailing the missed payrolls and a $4.6 million loan Pathak took from the startup in 2025, the chief executive addressed the issue in an all-hands meeting, according to the people.

Pathak defended the arrangement by telling workers that he had plowed his own money into the startup and takes only a nominal salary, the people said.

The co-founder also told employees who were in dire financial straits to contact human resources for help covering personal bills, the people added.

"I'm burning through savings just to afford my apartment," one employee told CNBC. "It's extremely unfortunate we're in this situation."

#123. Will I get the £100 energy payment and when will I receive it?

Up to 20 million households in England will get a Â£100 payment to help with their energy bills this winter, Chancellor John Healey has announced in his Budget.

Here's what you need to know about the payment.

For many people, the Â£100 on offer is a lot of money, but it's worth considering the context.

A typical energy bill is forecast to jump by Â£443 a year from January, according to consultants Cornwall Insight.

Therefore many people will still feel worse off despite the payment.

Those living in properties which are in council tax bands A to D in England will get the payment, the government has said.

As such, it's worth checking which council tax band your home is in, external. If you live in England, you can use the search function by typing your postcode into the box.

If you get a paper council tax bill, your band will also be printed on there.

The chancellor said the policy was for England but that funding would be given to Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland to make the payment as well.

The Scottish and Welsh governments have confirmed they will use the funding to introduce equivalent or similar payment schemes.

The Northern Irish government does not have the legal authority to make the Â£100 payments unless it agrees its budget, which it has failed to do since the start of this financial year.

Your pay, energy bills and savings: How the Budget affects you

If you pay your council tax by direct debit, the money will be paid directly into your bank account.

Those who don't pay by direct debit will be contacted by their council and given the choice to receive the Â£100 directly into their bank account or as credit on their council tax bill.

The government will ask councils to make payments from 4 January, and the scheme will be open until the end of May.

Students living in halls will not get the payment because student halls are not in council tax bands A to D.

However, if you're a student living in a property that is in council tax bands A to D, as in a private home or flat let out by a landlord, then you will get the payment.

Students do not pay council tax so the local authority will be responsible for finding a way to give them the payments.

This is also the case for those who do not pay council tax because of another kind of exemption, or if you do pay council tax but you do it by cash or some other way.

The government said it would give councils guidance on how local authorities should do this in due course.

The government has said the support is to help households with the cost of energy.

But the money will appear directly in people's bank accounts so you could choose to spend it on something else, while some people may choose to receive the Â£100 as a credit on their council tax bill.

The chancellor said the money for the Â£100 cash payments is coming from a tax rise on banks.

The surcharge on banks' corporation tax will rise from 3% to 6% for 15 months from 1 January 2027 to 31 March 2028.

#121. Peterson: Here’s what Bessent’s about-face on Jensen Huang reveals about Trump and China

For a minute there, it seemed like someone in the Trump administration might actually be willing to stand up to China.

For about a week and a half, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was the face of an emerging harder line on Nvidia over the potential risks its co-founder Jensen Huang's affairs posed to national security. Bessent was giving voice to China hawks who had felt sidelined by the Trump administration's willingness to sell artificial intelligence chips to  China.

They see China's AI program as a major threat, and want to restrain its growth by cutting off the supply of advanced chips to China. The resignation of Huang, who pressed the Trump Administration to allow the exports, seemed to signal that the hawks had found new influence in Washington.

By this weekend, Bessent had softened his public resistance to Huang's reinstatement. That came just in time for Nvidia's board to reappoint Huang as CEO at President Donald Trump's urging.

Whether any real damage was done to national security isn't clear. But it is safe to say that the incoming Democratic majority in Congress won't take Trump at his word when he said on CNBC Monday that the Department of Justice "did a pretty good investigation" that exonerated Huang.

Congress will look deeply into the worries Bessent himself raised about Huang's acknowledged romantic relationship with a woman The New York Times and others have reported was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia's board said it had received assurances from top U.S. officials and had concluded his return wasn't a national security risk.

A top concern is whether China was able to use the affair to help divert Nvidia's high-end artificial intelligence chips toward helping its military. Bessent in a Nov. 13 interview with CNBC outlined the stakes.

"Any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts the — it hurts our great companies and in the AI race it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Bessent said that his department and others had wanted to blacklist Megaspeed, the company previously run by Alice Huang, Jensen Huang's paramour. But "Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The Commerce Department is headed by Secretary Howard Lutnick, Bessent's rival.

Putting Huang back at Nvidia wasn't originally Bessent's preference. He said in a Fox interview on Nov. 15 that the board should "go outside the company" to find a new leader. But by this past weekend, Bessent had apparently gotten on board with Trump's wishes.

"Jensen is on the outside now," Bessent told CBS on Sunday, in trying to explain his apparent policy shift.

Commerce has since taken steps to tighten some restrictions meant to prevent U.S. chips from helping China advance in the AI race.

But there is bipartisan worry about the Nvidia episode. "I think we need to catalog the damage that's been done," Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., told The Wall Street Journal. He said Lutnick had not responded to letters he sent saying the administration should be "taking the threats of communist China more seriously."

But Trump has clearly put an upper limit on how aggressively anyone in his administration can push back on China. The president said Monday that he was swayed by the pageantry in his visit to China last week.

Xi "met me at the plane, which is something they've never done, which was a sign," Trump said.

Trump said he felt like he needed to intervene to back Huang because the stock market was plummeting in the wake of Nvidia's troubles — a development he also blamed on Democrats' midterm victory.

"I watched our country lose $5 trillion because of a bad election," he said. "If I didn't get involved, you'd probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come." Now, its stock performance will be "unbelievable."

Bessent on Sunday played down the market risks. The company "continues to be fantastically profitable, and there is near unlimited demand for their chips worldwide," he told CBS.

Lutnick has embraced Trump's transactional urges. He has backed deals in which the U.S. takes equity stakes in companies such as Intel, saying the taxpayer deserves some of the upside.

Lutnick in January said he had heard Huang pitch Trump on allowing Nvidia to sell its relatively more advanced H200 chips into China.

"There are strong — two sides. We shouldn't sell them any or we should sell them." Where does he stand? "Right in the middle," Lutnick told Bloomberg. Trump decided to go ahead with the deal.

Bessent has said he is "neither a hawk nor a dove" on China, and is simply trying to manage the ultra-sensitive relationship between the world's two biggest economies. But the upshot of the Huang affair seems to be that Bessent wasn't able to sway the president on a matter that seemed cut-and-dried just days ago.

Trump said he discussed whether Huang should return to Nvidia with Lutnick, Bessent and Vice President JD Vance. "Howard Lutnick was very good. Very, he's very professional," Trump said on CNBC Monday.

"I call them the opposites, Scott and Howard," Trump added, before declaring them "both good."

#120. Huawei found guilty of stealing trade secrets but acquitted of racketeering

Defence lawyer Brian Heberlig makes closing arguments at the Huawei Technologies trial at Brooklyn federal court on November 17.

Huawei has been found guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets and bank fraud, but not guilty of the more serious overarching racketeering charge in a landmark trial in the US.

In a two-month trial, prosecutors accused the Chinese technology and telecommunications giant of lying, cheating and stealing to gain any competitive advantage.

Huawei's defence team argued that prosecutors had decided Huawei was a criminal organisation and built its case from there, but the firm's success was due to its own innovation.

In a statement, Huawei said the racketeering acquittal "confirms that these allegations lack factual basis and vindicates our consistent position over the past eight years".

Jurors in a Brooklyn court found Huawei guilty of 10 out of 12 counts, which also included conspiracy to violate US sanctions.

However the jury declined to convict Huawei of the racketeering conspiracy charge, which required members to find the firm acted like a criminal enterprise.

Federal prosecutors first announced the charges against Huawei in 2019, after the dramatic arrest in late 2018 of senior executive Meng Wanzhou in Canada.

Meng, also known as Cathy Meng, was held for three years in Canada before she was released to return to China through a deal with US prosecutors.

During closing arguments last week, assistant US attorney Meredith Arfa told jurors Huawei spent 20 years doing whatever it took to expand its business, including lying to banks about its business dealings in Iran and stealing technology secrets from rival firms to ensure it had a competitive edge.

However, defence attorney Brian Heberlig said Huawei had grown into business with operations in 170 countries and more than 200,000 staff through hard work.

Following the verdict, Huawei said the five trade secret-related charges "were all civil matters that were resolved over the past two decades".

"No court ever found Huawei liable for willful intellectual property misappropriation, and the company was never ordered to pay damages for trade secret misappropriation," the company said.

"We will thoroughly review the relevant verdicts and take all necessary legal actions to defend our rights and interests."

#118. ESPN, NFL to show Super Bowl 61 in Imax theaters

Super Bowl 61 will be telecast live in hundreds of Imax theaters in February via a partnership with the NFL and Disney's ESPN, which has the broadcast rights to the 2027 game.

It marks the largest Imax live event in the company's history and the first time that the Super Bowl will be available in the format, according to a news release.

The four-hour event on Feb. 14 will include ESPN's main Super Bowl game telecast, as well as Jay-Z's halftime performance and pregame and postgame coverage.

Tickets will be available for theaters in the United States and select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil. Tickets are expected to go on sale in January. Pricing was not disclosed.

"Watching the Super Bowl with your fellow fans is one of the great shared experiences in sports, and this first-of-its-kind collaboration with the NFL and IMAX takes it to a historic new scale," said Ken Potrock, president of major event integration for The Walt Disney Co.

In addition to ESPN channels, this season's championship game will also be available on streaming platform Disney+ as well as Disney's broadcast network ABC.

#117. US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

US Attorney General Todd Blanche has backed Nvidia as a "good corporate citizen" while refusing to comment on whether the AI chipmaker was under federal investigation.

Blanche said he had no information about newly reinstated CEO Jensen Huang's security clearance status, as lawmakers demand answers about President Donald Trump's involvement in Huang's comeback.

Huang was given his job back less than two weeks after being forced to resign over revelations of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy.

Asked several times on Tuesday if the justice department or FBI had investigated Huang, Blanche responded: "I don't know what you're talking about."

Huang is still set to appear before Congress next week, where he will be questioned about national security concerns over his relationship with an alleged China spy who led Megaspeed, a Singapore-based corporate customer of Nvidia.

The US commerce department has also announced it was preparing to blacklist the tech company Megaspeed, which has been suspected of helping to divert advanced chips to China.

In a joint statement last week, the commerce and justice departments said they began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

But speaking to reporters on Tuesday, Blanche said the latter department has "hundreds, if not more" export investigations occurring at any one time.

"So when you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about," he said.

Asked if he was satisfied Nvidia had been co-operative with any efforts to get to the bottom of whether the company or Huang potentially knew about any chips being diverted to China, Blanche said: "I have no reason to stand up here and say Nvidia is anything but a good corporate citizen.

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target or was subject to some criminal investigation or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that the president is saying to the American people.

"It shouldn't be what my message would be either."

While Nvidia's stock market value surged on the announcement of Huang's reappointment, some of the gains were reversed in volatile Tuesday trading.

The company's share price closed down nearly 6% - still well above where it ended last week. On Monday, Nvidia stock rocketed more than 31% following news of Huang's return.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

The company's board reinstated Huang on Monday in a shock U-turn. Harvey Jones, chairman of Nvidia's board, said Huang's return was in the "best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers".

The board said it had received input from senior levels of the US government and was confident that Huang's reinstatement posed no national security risk.

On Monday, Trump said he spoke to the company's directors and convinced them to bring back Huang.

Huang's reinstatement provoked bipartisan anger.

Republican congressman Don Bacon said Nvidia should have "ignored the Administration's inexplicable desire to save Huang".

Republican Senator Pete Ricketts said Huang's behaviour "doesn't absolve Nvidia of answering for national security concerns and unaccounted-for chips".

Democratic congressman Robert Garcia, the incoming chairman of the House oversight committee, said Trump's "direct involvement" with Nvidia's decision "raises serious national security and corruption questions".

Democratic Senator Ruben Gallego said Congress needed to "investigate what stock positions took place before the announcement".
