#115. Congress shreds Nvidia's 'outrageous' decision to bring back Huang: 'Failure of character'

Members of Congress from both parties on Monday expressed outrage and incredulity, and doubled down on their calls for investigations, after Nvidia announced it has reinstated Jensen Huang as its president and CEO.

The resurrection of Huang, who had resigned from the artificial intelligence behemoth just 11 days earlier, raises more questions about the company's decision-making and the Trump administration's national security assessments, on top of existing concerns about Nvidia's advanced AI chips being diverted to China, the lawmakers said.

"It's inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO," Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., a China hawk who is retiring from Congress, told CNBC in a phone interview. "To me it's a failure of character."

President Donald Trump told CNBC in an extended interview later Monday morning that he personally intervened to convince Nvidia's board to bring Huang back, saying, "I made the deal."

Bacon, chairman of the House Subcommittee on Cyber, Information Technologies, and Innovation, said he wants the next Congress to conduct thorough oversight, while questioning the Trump administration's motives.

"Somebody's responsible for this, and it may be" Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department is responsible for setting U.S. export controls on AI chips, Bacon said. "But I think we should get to the bottom of this."

Huang, who is scheduled to testify before multiple committees on Capitol Hill starting Dec. 1, is "going to get high scrutiny in a bipartisan way when he comes next week," Bacon said.

Huang's closed-door appearance before a trio of House committees is on the schedule, as the panels seek answers on the national security implications of Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, MS NOW reported. The Senate banking committee announced after Huang's reinstatement that he will appear for a public hearing Dec. 3.

John Fisher of Trident, a spokesman for Huang, declined to comment on the Nvidia CEO's scheduled appearances.

"He better be there," Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., a member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told CNBC about Huang's scheduled Dec. 1 appearance. His reinstatement will only intensify congressional probes into Nvidia and its government connections, Amo predicted.

"I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query, and it makes Howard Lutnick and his dealings — supporting his nepo children's operation at Cantor Fitzgerald — party to inquiry as well as any other administration official that has facilitated a status quo in which chips can be sold to China or Chinese operated intermediaries who don't intend good things for the American people," said Amo, referring to the financial firm run by Lutnick's sons that has business ties to Nvidia.

A Cantor spokesperson didn't comment directly when asked about Amo's comments, saying the firm "holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia" and noting its Nvidia positions "arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients."

The Commerce Department didn't immediately respond to requests to comment on Bacon's and Amo's remarks.

Nvidia in a statement Monday said it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

David Rudenstine, a constitutional law professor and national security scholar at Cardozo School of Law, said the frenzy around Huang's comeback is secondary to lingering national security concerns."The real responsibility for national security is with the administration and not with the CEO of a private company," Rudenstine told CNBC."You're opposed to China getting the chips. You recognize that the intermediary companies are sending chips to China, and you don't put a stop to it. I don't get it," he said.

The CEO's reinstatement, while celebrated by the White House and in Silicon Valley, led to heightened calls on Capitol Hill to seek answers on AI chip exports to China and what the Trump administration's role has been in facilitating that.

"We are sending high-end computer chips to China, and yet we're saying we want to win the AI war. It's a contradiction, and it's a failure of leadership in the White House," Bacon told CNBC.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, told CNBC in an emailed statement that Huang's return "proves that under Trump, Vance, and Rubio, national security is for sale to the highest bidder."

"We will all pay the price of letting China plunder America's cutting edge technology. Democrats will be pushing for accountability for anyone in the administration that put corrupt deals over our country's security," Wyden, who is also a Senate Intelligence Committee member, said.

Trump in a Truth Social post Monday added that Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Lawmakers, who have already launched multiple investigations related to Huang, Nvidia and related entities, quickly filed in to question those conclusions.

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., a close Trump ally and a former CEO, criticized the move and called for a congressional briefing "as soon as possible" on Huang's reinstatement.

"Nvidia's board is free to vote however they want, but the federal government owes the American people answers about what actually happened," Scott told CNBC in an emailed statement. "This looks terrible on its face, and it drives home the need for Congress to be informed on the full impact of Jensen Huang's actions on our national security and economy."

Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., a member of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, told CNBC that it's still not clear to what degree Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy might have compromised national security.

"The fact that the president of the United States is involved in putting him back in there, presumably because maybe he has stock or he just cares about the stock market, it's really reached a bizarre place," Peters said. "And obviously that man has not learned anything from the election about what people think of him, or he doesn't care."

Monday's move also gave fuel to lawmakers who have already questioned Trump, his administration or Nvidia.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement via the Senate Banking Committee, where she is the top Democrat and is expected to become chair in January.

Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, more tersely questioned the official assurances that Huang poses no risks to national security.

"No national security risk? Based on what?" he wrote on X.

Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi, D-Ill., who serves on the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, in an X post called Nvidia's decision to reinstate Huang "outrageous."

"We still don't know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia's own board has completed its investigation," wrote Krishnamoorthi, who is also a member of the House Oversight and Intelligence panels.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan, Garrett Downs and Luke Fountain contributed to this report.

#113. Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

As the market bell rang out at the Nasdaq today, the Nvidia super-bounce faded.

Its shares slid and are down almost 30% over the past six months, well before the scandal involving Jensen Huang emerged earlier this month.

The legitimate fears about the staying power of the tech boom pre-dated Huang's exit. They have not gone away with his return.

Investors are jittery. Already Anthropic is now trading notably lower than last week's stock market flotation. The firm is the first purely AI company to be publicly listed, and is being seen as a big test for continued investor appetite for the technology.

President Trump's extraordinary phone call to CNBC taking credit for telling the board to reinstate Huang showed the extent of the operation - of some in Silicon Valley and within the US administration - to get him back at the helm.

Among the truly astonishing reflections from President Trump was a suggestion that a two-day investigation of allegations was done and had cleared Huang of national security concerns.

The President said his remarkable move to intervene was because Huang is a "national treasure" who has the next generation of AI chips "in his head". Trump says this is why he needed to act.

In markets, those sceptical about the AI boom say they have never seen an operation to prop up a stock market boom like this. The bond vigilantes and short sellers are now up against Silicon Valley and the White House.

Investors are trading not revenue numbers but levels of political support at a time when US voters have just delivered a backlash against AI data centres.

Against this backdrop, the recovery of the central cash-generating foundation of the tech boom - Nvidia and its $400bn (Â£304bn) annual sales of advanced chips - looks politically fragile.

This worsens other uncertainties and risks, such as Nvidia's central role in the web of financing, borrowing and cross-ownership to support every corner of the build out of data centres.

The brief interim Nvidia chief executive Colette Kress had already signalled a rowing back from elements of this strategy last week. Where is that now?

The fall back in Anthropic's share price also raises questions about OpenAI's planned listing on the stock exchange. Ultimately these two companies might be the most critical part of all this.

They remain valuable companies with phenomenal technology relied on by the world's biggest companies. The real question is whether that reliance eases, as corporations eschew expensive frontier models for cheaper generic models offered by China.

In turn, all this could be affected by the political backlash, to which the Nvidia psychodrama now contributes.

It is simply not normal to have trillion dollar swings in the value of individual companies.

The extraordinary volatility shows the fragile mood of investors.

#111. Some U.S. food banks see demand top pandemic levels

During the Covid-19 pandemic, Greg Silverman watched an MTA bus driver pull over on Manhattan's Upper West Side, let his passengers off and run across the street toward him.

Silverman, who runs New York nonprofit West Side Campaign Against Hunger, initially thought the driver wanted to use the bathroom at the church where the food distribution was taking place. Instead, the man asked, "Can I get some food?"

"He was a full-time employee," Silverman said. "You are an essential personnel, making the city survive and you need food."

Now, Silverman said, his organization has seen demand surpass even the levels it observed during that time of crisis when the U.S. economy ground to a halt — and many of the people now seeking food also have jobs. Silverman expects the nonprofit to distribute as much as 6 million pounds of food this year.

"The height of the pandemic was not the height of need, by any means," he said.

The pressure on the emergency food system extends beyond New York. Nationally, use of charitable food programs remains elevated as sustained inflation makes it harder for Americans to afford food, housing and healthcare. Higher prices come at the same time that the federal government has restricted food aid.

The Department of Agriculture's latest annual report found that 13.7% of U.S. households, roughly 18.3 million households, experienced food insecurity in 2024. That rate was significantly higher than in every year from 2016 through 2021.

The pandemic initially brought a surge in demand for charitable food programs, which was followed by government support. In the years that followed, pandemic-era policies such as expanded Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, or SNAP, benefits, the enhanced child tax credit and school meal programs phased out as inflation spiked.

"This is not because you can't go to work," said City Harvest President Carlos Rodriguez. "Primarily, your work and your wages are just not keeping up with the way costs have gone up in the last five years alone."

City Harvest, which works with food providers across the five boroughs of New York, told CNBC 1 in 5 New Yorkers is currently experiencing food insecurity, or limited access to enough food because of a lack of money or other resources. That is up from 1 in 6 a year earlier, and 1 in 8 before the pandemic.

Average monthly visits to food pantries and soup kitchens in New York are also 85% higher than before the pandemic, said Rodriguez.

In the Federal Reserve's October Beige Book, the central bank's New York regional branch described a sharp rise in first-time food pantry users. One nonprofit leader told the Federal Reserve Bank of New York that the strain was comparable to the pandemic, "but without the same philanthropic and government support."

Data from Plentiful, a technology platform that connects people with food pantries and tracks their usage, also points to growing demand and limited resources.

"For pantries using our reservation and pre-registration features, we see that the overwhelming majority of those are at or over capacity," said Catherine Compitello, CEO of Plentiful. "Demand is growing while supply is not."

First-time users of Plentiful in New York City rose roughly 56% from August to October, Compitello said. It was the second-largest fall surge since 2016; the only larger one came during last year's federal government shutdown, when delayed SNAP benefits drove more people to seek food assistance.

Compitello said that when families have trouble affording essentials, they often prioritize healthcare, transportation, childcare and utilities, leaving food as the expense they cut back on.

Demand for food banks is rising in other regions of the country, and nonprofits are facing their own operational challenges as they try to meet it.

A spokesperson for the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank told CNBC the organization's diesel bill more than doubled in September, to roughly $75,000, adding to the cost of delivering food and running refrigerated trucks.

Demand in the region is also spiking. Distribution in the Los Angeles area for January through September totaled 116 million pounds of food and grocery products — the equivalent of 93 million meals — and slightly higher than a near-record year in 2025, according to the Los Angeles Regional Food Bank.

Federal food aid changes have exacerbated the problems. Rodriguez of City Harvest described SNAP as a first line of defense against hunger, saying the program can provide as many as nine meals for every one meal the organization provides. But City Harvest has estimated at least 8.7% of SNAP recipients in New York City lost benefits over the past year, after congressional Republicans and President Donald Trump approved work certification requirements and changes to eligibility.

The pressure is also mounting in Los Angeles County, where roughly 260,000 CalFresh recipients are subject to expanded federal work requirements that took effect in June. CalFresh is California's version of SNAP.

That does not mean all 260,000 will lose benefits, since people who meet the work requirements or qualify for an exemption can remain eligible. But a Los Angeles Regional Food Bank spokesperson told CNBC the changes add another layer of uncertainty for households already struggling with food costs.

In Atlanta, the pressure extends beyond the city itself to the surrounding region. The Atlanta Community Food Bank serves about 270,000 households a month, or nearly 700,000 people, through partner pantries across North Georgia. President and CEO Kyle Waide said the number of people it serves has jumped more than 70% in five years.

"Much of that increase is among people whose incomes are too high to qualify for federal benefit programs," said Waide.

Under current SNAP guidelines in Georgia, most households must have gross monthly income at or below 130% of the federal poverty level to qualify. They must also meet a separate net income test. For fiscal 2027, which began Oct. 1, the gross income limit is $2,960 a month for a household of three, or about $35,520 annually, and $3,575 a month for a household of four, or $42,900 a year.

More than 60% of Atlanta Community Food Bank clients have household incomes above the cutoff for SNAP eligibility, Waide said, generally in the mid-to-upper $30,000 range depending on household size. The food bank does not collect everyone's income, he said, but the figure is based on survey estimates.

Given those dynamics, Waide said he does not expect the need to ease.

"We're preparing for the level of demand to stay where it is today, or even increase more," Waide said. "We don't see a big source of relief coming anytime soon."

#108. Anthropic closes below $83 IPO price, but analysts are still largely positive

Shares of Anthropic closed down 6.5% on Tuesday, sending the stock below the artificial intelligence company's $83 IPO price.

The AI lab's stock has been sinking steadily since opening at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, after Anthropic raised $81.3 billion in the largest offering of all time. Investors eagerly awaited Anthropic's debut in hopes that the company would revitalize the broader AI trade, which has been volatile in recent weeks over concerns about the feasibility of a multitrillion-dollar infrastructure buildout.

Chip stocks Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Intel, as well as neoclouds CoreWeave, IREN and Nebius, all fell during intraday trading on Tuesday. The market is still reeling after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, a dramatic reversal from his ouster earlier this month. Nvidia shares surged more than 30% on Monday, but pared those gains Tuesday.

Analysts at Canaccord Genuity said Tuesday that they're focused on Anthropic's long-term potential. They said they believe that the company is building "one of the most important enterprise technology franchises of the AI era" and that the "opportunity is considerably larger than traditional software spending."

Canaccord Genuity initiated coverage of Anthropic with a buy rating and a $135 price target.

"The risks are unusually large, particularly around infrastructure commitments and the durability of competitive differentiation, but we believe the combination of exceptional growth, emerging earnings capacity, and an attractive entry valuation support a compelling investment opportunity," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

Wedbush analysts initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating and a $120 price target on Monday. They said Anthropic went public in a "tumultuous market" as debate around the AI buildout continues, but that they expect the company to navigate the competitive environment and "capture a uniquely large addressable market."

"While there's going to continue to be many ups and downs, and winners and losers as this ecosystem gets built, in our view there is no real question that it does get built over time," the analysts wrote in a Monday note. "The genie is out of the bottle. And ANT is a strong play on that theme."

Analysts at Oppenheimer initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating on Tuesday, and they set a $150 price target for the stock.

They said their "bullish stance" on the company stems from their belief that Anthropic will capture growing enterprise AI spending, that its improving compute efficiency lowers cost per task and that its multi-chip strategy optimizes price performance. The analysts added that Anthropic's focus on the enterprise also relies less on consumer subsidies, and that rising customer adoption creates "a substantial runway" for the company.

"While competition and compute commitments remain risks, we believe Anthropic can translate its leadership into durable growth and operating leverage," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

#105. Best Buy reports comparable sales growth of 6.1%, highest in five years

Best Buy on Tuesday beat Wall Street expectations on the top and bottom lines for its third fiscal quarter and raised its full-year guidance.

The consumer electronics retailer reported comparable sales growth of 6.1%, a significant beat over expectations of 2.3%, according to market intelligence platform StreetAccount. The company said that growth was its highest in five years. It reported a domestic comparable sales increase of 6.2% and international comparable sales growth of 4.9%.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

Best Buy also raised its guidance for the rest of the year. It's now expecting full-year revenue of between $43 billion and $43.3 billion compared with a previous expectation of between $42.3 billion and $42.8 billion. It's expecting comparable sales to be up 3.4% to 4% compared with prior guidance of between 1.9% and 3%.

The earnings report is the company's first since Bonfig took over as CEO from Corie Barry at the beginning of the month.

Bonfig said the retailer saw overperformance in both computing and gaming for the quarter, driven by recent game launches and a targeted product assortment.

Looking ahead into the holiday season, he said the Best Buy consumer remains "resilient," but that they are focused on getting value wherever they can.

"We did see not only our current customers continue to be engaged, but we actually did see more new customers come into the brand, as well as we had some customers that we re-engaged as well," Bonfig said. "So those are really, really positive signs for us in Q3, and all of that was factored into the way that we guided for Q4 and the rest of the year."

Here's how the company performed in the period ended Oct. 31 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Best Buy reported net income of $353 million, or $1.66 per share, compared with $140 million, or 66 cents per share, the year prior. Adjusting for one-time items, including restructuring charges, Best Buy posted adjusted earnings of $1.62 per share.

Revenue rose to $10.24 billion from $9.67 billion in the year-ago period. Best Buy reported domestic revenue of $9.42 billion and international revenue of $820 million.

Tuesday's earnings are the latest in a series of strong reports from the retailer, which has been instituting a turnaround strategy to return to growth after getting hit by declining foot traffic, lower consumer sentiment, tariffs and memory costs.

Shares of Best Buy are up nearly 45% this year.

Bonfig told CNBC last quarter that the launch of "Grand Theft Auto VI" in the current quarter is expected to drive sales in the gaming category, which has been seeing gains over the past year. Last month, Best Buy launched a new gaming experience for customers featuring gaming content, technology and special offers, among other features.

"I think there'll still be opportunities for gaming to continue to drive our business moving forward," Bonfig told CNBC on Tuesday.

#104. Consumer confidence shoots up after Democrats' midterm victories — though inflation worries persist

Consumers grew significantly more optimistic in November in the wake of the midterm elections, with a growing number of Americans now seeing improvement in the labor market and expecting inflation to cool.

The Conference Board survey released Tuesday showed improvement across the board, with the headline confidence index jumping to 103.3, up 11.4 points from October and much better than the Dow Jones consensus forecast for 92.2.

Confidence among Democrats and independent voters improved sharply, the Conference Board said, while confidence among Republican respondents fell slightly. Still, confidence among Republicans remained the highest among the three groups.

While respondents "continued to express frustration with high prices and rising costs," they also "cited hope for the general economy," said Fergus McCormick, the board's senior economist for global indicators.

The survey comes with the Federal Reserve continuing to try to bring down inflation, which has run above the central bank's 2% target for some 5½ years. The Fed is expected to raise its benchmark interest rate by a quarter percentage point when it meets again in two weeks.

Despite the lingering worries about the cost of living — a centerpiece issue during the midterms — the survey's present situation and expectations indexes both posted strong gains. Inflation expectations for 12 months from now fell for the second straight month but remained high at 5.7%.

The survey's closely watched gap between jobs considered "plentiful" versus "hard to get" widened to 12.4 percentage points. The "plentiful" side rose to 30.8% from 26.2% while "hard to get" fell to 18.4%, down 3 percentage points.

The survey results are the second in the past week to reveal that Americans are growing more optimistic about the economy. The University of Michigan's November survey, released Friday, showed a 7.3% increase since October, with a similar partisan split.

#97. Gold is doing something strange. Here's one way to play it

Gold has stopped trading like a haven and started trading more like a market index this year. The past week has underscored it. Fears around AI, a hawkish Fed, and a stronger dollar sent many investors rushing to raise cash, and gold was sold along with stocks. GLD (SPDR Gold Shares) and the S&P 500 both slid around 9%. Gold then shared in the market's Monday rebound, with GLD up about 3% as the S&P 500 jumped nearly 5%.

Over long timeframes, gold's returns tend to move independently of the S&P 500's. From 2004 to 2025, the correlation between their daily returns has been just 0.05 (+1 means the two move in lockstep and -1 means they move in opposite directions). That relationship fluctuates, and 2026 has seen an unusually strong link. The correlation has been about 0.41 so far in 2026. Its rolling three-month reading peaked near 0.80 in June and now sits at 0.54, still higher than about 95% of rolling three-month readings since 2005. Since the end of August, GLD has fallen on 77% of the S&P 500's down days, compared with roughly half historically.

The options market is also behaving unusually. Gold options typically price calls richer than puts (call skew). Like other commodities, gold is prone to sharp rallies, and investors use calls to bet on them or hedge against macroeconomic risks. However, gold options have mostly shown the reverse this year, with puts currently trading richer than calls (put skew). It's a sign investors are paying a premium for protection against further declines, which is unusual for gold and more typical of stock options.

Those richer puts come as uncertainty around gold runs high heading into December. The PCE inflation report arrives Wednesday, followed by the jobs report next week and the Fed's decision and CPI the week after. GLD's implied volatility, a measure of how much the options market expects the fund to move, is now higher than on about 80% of days over the past year. Options are pricing in an expected move of about 6%, or roughly $20, by Dec. 18. With gold options pricier than usual and puts unusually rich, that presents an interesting setup for traders willing to sell premium.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the Dec. 18 310/300 put spread and 360/370 call spread in GLD for a credit of roughly $2.24.

This is a directionally neutral trade. Gold has been swinging with the market and is down about 33% from its January high. With the Fed and inflation data ahead, it's hard to say whether gold has bottomed or has further to fall into mid-December. A neutral position can collect the elevated premium without picking a direction, and the richer put side adds more.

The short strikes sit just outside the range the options market is pricing for Dec. 18, roughly 7% to 8% from Monday's close. That leaves room for a continued selloff or an extended rebound. The trade reaches its maximum profit of $224 if GLD closes between $310 and $360 on Dec. 18. It carries a 62% theoretical probability of profit and can show a gain before expiration if GLD remains relatively rangebound.

The maximum loss is capped by the long options and reaches $776 if GLD closes below $300 or above $370, 10% to 11% from Monday's close. Defining risk is especially important with the upcoming catalysts and recent price volatility in gold. GLD has had 30 daily moves of more than 3% this year, including a 10.3% drop on Jan. 30. The trade breaks even at $307.76 on the downside and $362.24 on the upside.

A close between $360 and $370 can assign the short call and leave a short position in the fund, so investors looking to avoid that should consider managing the trade before expiration. If GLD finishes between $300 and $310, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $310. After the credit, that's a cost basis of about $307.76, roughly 8% below Monday's close.

#96. Retail turnarounds are taking hold just in time for the holiday season

Once-struggling retailers are entering the holiday quarter with improved performance and sales momentum as turnaround efforts begin to resonate with shoppers.

A handful of earnings reports on Tuesday pointed to a mix of factors behind the strong results, including a resilient consumer, stronger execution and demand for the right products at the right price.

Kohl's reported comparable sales growth of 1.2% for the period ended Oct. 31 and raised its full-year outlook as CEO Michael Bender said customers "have remained resilient" but are "migrating to value with their purchases."

He tied improvement to the company's work on product assortment, store experience and value.

Best Buy also on Tuesday posted its highest comparable sales growth in five years, at 6.1%, which prompted the electronics retailer to raise its full-year outlook.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC in an interview. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

The company also credited execution, market share gains and a compelling product cycle alongside growing customer demand.

At Dick's Sporting Goods, quarterly comparable sales rose 2.6% at the core business and 0.5% at Foot Locker. CEO Lauren Hobart said "all income demographics grew" and the company "didn't see trade-down from best to better or better to good."

She similarly pointed to new products and market share gains within the category as contributing to the strong quarter. She also highlighted continued improvement at Foot Locker.

All three companies have been undergoing efforts to boost sales and get the right products on shelves to meet customer demand.

Target, likewise undergoing revitalization efforts, last week reported comparable sales growth of 5.6% in the third quarter, fueled by a 5% increase in comparable traffic. The retailer raised its full-year outlook.

CEO Michael Fiddelke at the time called the traffic gain "a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often."

Even Gap CEO Richard Dickson told CNBC the company has moved from "rebuild mode" to "turnaround mode" at its Old Navy banner, a sign of progress, albeit not a broad-based portfolio recovery.

Michael Baker, a retail analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC he sees both a resilient consumer and company-specific improvements as driving sector strength.

"Retail sales have been more resilient than most think," Baker said. His analysis showed retail sales, excluding autos and gas, rose 5.1% in the third quarter on a nonseasonally adjusted basis — the strongest quarter since the pandemic.

But he cautioned that growth appears to be coming more from transaction size than traffic, with inflation, promotions and higher units per transaction driving up spend.

Dollar Tree, which reported better-than-expected earnings on Tuesday morning, posted a 3.8% increase to traffic, accelerating from 0.4% growth in the prior quarter, suggesting more shoppers may be bargain hunting. Comparable sales at the retailer increased 4.6% during the period.

"Consumers are under pressure, and they are increasingly selective about where and how they spend," said Dollar Tree CEO Michael Creedon on the company's earnings call. "When under pressure, shoppers prioritize value and buy closer to need."

Comparable sales of everyday essentials rose 8.9% at Dollar Tree stores in the third quarter, while sales of nonessential items were essentially flat, the company said.

— CNBC's Laya Neelakandan contributed to this report.

#92. Saudi national charged in OpenAI bugging to be released on $1 million bond

Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room, will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.

Alfozan, 35, is expected to be released on Wednesday and be fitted with an ankle monitor, according to his attorney, David Rizk.

"We are simply grateful that the Court made the right decision to release Mr. Alfozan and now we look forward to defending the case," Rizk said in a text message after a hearing before U.S. District Court Magistrate Judge Alex Tse.

Tse's ruled in favor of a release motion by Rizk filed last week that said prosecutors had admitted they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

The San Francisco U.S. Attorney's Office opposed the former OpenAI engineer's release, saying in its response that he remains a flight risk due to his "considerable resources and ties to Saudi Arabia."

Alfozan's release request "blithely dismisses the seriousness" of the charge he faces, and "appears to presume, without support, that the government's investigation is complete," prosecutors wrote.

Alfozan is accused of concealing a recording device in the artificial intelligence company's conference room to secretly record discussions.

Prosecutors said they have found hundreds of audio files on Alfozan's personal computer, "includingdozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets."

Jury selection for Alfozan's trial is scheduled to begin Jan. 20.

OpenAI did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Alfozan's planned release.

— CNBC's Ashley Capoot contributed to this article.
